Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the given point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
   - Is as close as possible to point (x,y) in terms of Euclidean distance from center
   - If multiple solutions exist, choose lexicographically smallest

## Mathematical Analysis

For a rectangle with dimensions (w,h):
- w = x₂ - x₁
- h = y₂ - y₁
- We need w/h = a/b, so w = k*a, h = k*b for some positive integer k

The center of the rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁ + x₁ + w)/2, (y₁ + y₁ + h)/2) = (x₁ + w/2, y₁ + h/2)

The distance from center to point (x,y) is:
d² = (x - (x₁ + w/2))² + (y - (y₁ + h/2))²

## Constraints

- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + w = x₁ + k*a
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + h = y₁ + k*b
- x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ = x₁ + k*a ≤ n, so x₁ ≤ n - k*a
- y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ = y₁ + k*b ≤ m, so y₁ ≤ m - k*b

## Solution Strategy

1. For each possible k, calculate valid x₁ and y₁ ranges
2. For each valid k, find the best center position (closest to (x,y))
3. Among all valid solutions, pick the one with minimum Euclidean distance, then lexicographically smallest

## Step-by-step Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # We need to find k such that w = k*a, h = k*b
    # Constraints:
    # x1 >= 0, x1 + k*a <= n => k*a <= n => k <= n/a
    # y1 >= 0, y1 + k*b <= m => k*b <= m => k <= m/b
    # x1 <= x <= x1 + k*a => x - k*a <= x1 <= x
    # y1 <= y <= y1 + k*b => y - k*b <= y1 <= y
    
    # So we need: 0 <= x - k*a <= x <= x + k*a <= n
    # And: 0 <= y - k*b <= y <= y + k*b <= m
    
    # This gives us: k >= (x - x) / a = 0 (always true)
    # k >= (x - n) / a (but we have tighter constraints)
    
    # From x1 >= 0: x - k*a >= 0 => k <= x/a
    # From x1 <= x: x - k*a <= x (always true)
    # From x1 >= 0 and x1 + k*a <= n: k*a <= n and x - k*a >= 0 => k <= min(x/a, n/a)
    
    # From y1 >= 0: y - k*b >= 0 => k <= y/b
    # From y1 <= y: y - k*b <= y (always true)
    # From y1 + k*b <= m: k*b <= m and y - k*b >= 0 => k <= min(y/b, m/b)
    
    # So k must satisfy: k <= min(x/a, n/a, y/b, m/b)
    # and k >= 1 (since k=0 gives degenerate rectangle)
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_dist_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Try different values of k
    # k must be at least 1
    # k must satisfy: k*a <= n and k*b <= m and k*a <= x and k*b <= y?
    # Actually: from x1 >= 0 and x1 <= x: we need x - k*a >= 0 => k <= x/a
    # And x1 + k*a <= n => k*a <= n => k <= n/a
    
    # So k <= min(x // a, n // a, y // b, m // b) but we need integer values
    
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    # Try all possible k values
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        w = k * a
        h = k * b
        
        # Calculate valid x1 range
        # x1 >= 0 and x1 <= x and x1 + w >= x and x1 + w <= n
        # x1 >= max(0, x - w) and x1 <= min(x, n - w)
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        
        # Calculate valid y1 range  
        # y1 >= 0 and y1 <= y and y1 + h >= y and y1 + h <= m
        # y1 >= max(0, y - h) and y1 <= min(y, m - h)
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        # If no valid positions for this k, skip
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For this k, we need to find x1, y1 that minimize distance to (x,y)
        # Center is at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
        # Distance squared: (x - (x1 + w/2))^2 + (y - (y1 + h/2))^2
        
        # We want to minimize this, so we should center around (x,y)
        # For x1: we want x1 + w/2 ≈ x, so x1 ≈ x - w/2
        # For y1: we want y1 + h/2 ≈ y, so y1 ≈ y - h/2
        
        x1_opt = x - w / 2
        y1_opt = y - h / 2
        
        # Round to nearest integers
        x1_candidate = int(round(x1_opt))
        y1_candidate = int(round(y1_opt))
        
        # But we need to ensure x1_candidate is in range [x1_min, x1_max]
        # and y1_candidate is in range [y1_min, y1_max]
        
        # Check if candidates are valid
        x1_candidate = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, x1_candidate))
        y1_candidate = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, y1_candidate))
        
        # We also need to check the boundaries and find the best among valid positions
        # Since the function is convex, checking the endpoints might be better
        
        # Try the optimal value and the endpoints
        candidates = []
        
        # Add the rounded value
        if x1_min <= x1_candidate <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_candidate <= y1_max:
            candidates.append((x1_candidate, y1_candidate))
        
        # Add boundaries
        if x1_min <= x1_max:
            candidates.append((x1_min, y1_min))
            candidates.append((x1_min, y1_max))
            candidates.append((x1_max, y1_min))
            candidates.append((x1_max, y1_max))
        
        # Add center point if it exists
        # Actually, we should check just the optimal value and boundaries
        
        # For each candidate, calculate distance and see which is best
        for x1_cand, y1_cand in candidates:
            x2 = x1_cand + w
            y2 = y1_cand + h
            
            # Check if valid
            if x1_cand < 0 or x2 > n or y1_cand < 0 or y2 > m:
                continue
                
            # Calculate distance squared to (x,y)
            center_x = x1_cand + w / 2
            center_y = y1_cand + h / 2
            dist_sq = (x - center_x)**2 + (y - center_y)**2
            
            # Check if this is better than current best
            if (best_dist_squared == float('inf') or 
                dist_sq < best_dist_squared or 
                (dist_sq == best_dist_squared and 
                 (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2) > (x1_cand, y1_cand, x2, y2))):
                best_dist_squared = dist_sq
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_cand, y1_cand, x2, y2
    
    # If we found no solution, we need to check if we missed something
    # The problem guarantees there's a solution, so we should always find one
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find a sub-rectangle in an n×m grid that:
1. Contains the point (x, y)
2. Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
3. Is as close as possible to the center of the grid (minimizing Euclidean distance from (x,y) to rectangle center)
4. If multiple solutions, choose lexicographically smallest

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b
2. This means: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)
3. We need to find valid ranges [x₁,x₂] and [y₁,y₂] such that:
   - 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ ≤ n
   - 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ ≤ m
   - b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parameterize the problem
Let's define:
- Width = w = x₂ - x₁
- Height = h = y₂ - y₁
- We need w/h = a/b, so w = (a/b)h

Since w and h must be integers, we need h to be a multiple of b, and w to be a multiple of a.

Let h = k×b for some positive integer k, then w = k×a.

### Step 2: Constraints
For x-direction: x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
- x₁ ≥ 0
- x₂ ≤ n
- x₂ - x₁ = w = k×a
- So: x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ = x₁ + k×a ≤ n, and x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a

For y-direction: y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂  
- y₁ ≥ 0  
- y₂ ≤ m  
- y₂ - y₁ = h = k×b  
- So: y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ = y₁ + k×b ≤ m, and y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k×b

### Step 3: Find valid k values
We need to find k such that:
- x₁ ≥ 0 → x₁ ≥ 0
- x₂ = x₁ + k×a ≤ n → x₁ ≤ n - k×a
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a → x - k×a ≤ x₁ ≤ x
- Similarly for y: y - k×b ≤ y₁ ≤ y and y₁ ≥ 0, y₁ ≤ m - k×b

So for x-direction: max(0, x - k×a) ≤ x₁ ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
For y-direction: max(0, y - k×b) ≤ y₁ ≤ min(y, m - k×b)

### Step 4: For each valid k, find optimal rectangle
For each valid k:
- x₁ range: [max(0, x - k×a), min(x, n - k×a)]
- y₁ range: [max(0, y - k×b), min(y, m - k×b)]

The center of rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁+x₁+w)/2, (y₁+y₁+h)/2) = (x₁ + w/2, y₁ + h/2)

Distance to (x,y): d² = (x₁ + w/2 - x)² + (y₁ + h/2 - y)²

We want to minimize this distance, then lexicographically.

### Step 5: Algorithm
1. Find valid k values
2. For each k, find valid x₁, y₁ ranges
3. For each valid (x₁,y₁), calculate distance and select the best one
4. Handle the lexicographic ordering

```python
def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_dist_squared = float('inf')
    
    # We need to find valid k values
    # k must satisfy: k*a <= n and k*b <= m
    # Also need valid ranges for x1 and y1
    
    # Maximum k value
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    # For each possible k
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        w = k * a
        h = k * b
        
        # Find valid x1 range
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        
        # Find valid y1 range  
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        # If no valid ranges, skip
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # Try all combinations in the ranges
        # But we need to find optimal one minimizing distance
        # Since the distance function is convex, we can find optimal x1, y1
        
        # For distance minimization: minimize (x1 + w/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + h/2 - y)^2
        # This is minimized when:
        # x1 + w/2 is as close as possible to x
        # y1 + h/2 is as close as possible to y
        
        # So optimal x1 should be as close as possible to x - w/2
        x1_opt = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, int(x - w/2)))
        x1_opt = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, x1_opt))
        
        # But we need to check both boundaries
        x1_candidates = [x1_min, x1_max]
        if x1_min < x1_max:
            x1_candidates.append(int(x - w/2))
        
        x1_candidates = [x for x in x1_candidates if x1_min <= x <= x1_max]
        
        y1_candidates = [y1_min, y1_max]
        if y1_min < y1_max:
            y1_candidates.append(int(y - h/2))
        
        y1_candidates = [y for y in y1_candidates if y1_min <= y <= y1_max]
        
        # Try all candidates
        for x1 in x1_candidates:
            for y1 in y1_candidates:
                x2 = x1 + w
                y2 = y1 + h
                
                # Check if this rectangle is valid
                if 0 <= x1 <= x <= x2 <= n and 0 <= y1 <= y <= y2 <= m:
                    center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2
                    center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2
                    dist_squared = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                    
                    if (dist_squared < best_dist_squared or 
                        (dist_squared == best_dist_squared and 
                         (best_x1 is None or (x1, y1, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)))):
                        best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                        best_dist_squared = dist_squared
    
    # If no solution found, we need to handle edge cases
    # But the problem guarantees at least one solution exists
    if best_x1 is None:
        # Fallback: try k=1
        w = a
        h = b
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            x1 = x1_min
```

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
best_dist_squared = float('inf')

# Maximum possible k
max_k = min(n // a, m // b)

# For each k, we need to find x1 and y1 that minimize distance
for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    w = k * a
    h = k * b
    
    # Valid x1 range: x1 <= x <= x1 + w, 0 <= x1 <= n - w
    x1_min = max(0, x - w)
    x1_max = min(x, n - w)
    
    # Valid y1 range: y1 <= y <= y1 + h, 0 <= y1 <= m - h  
    y1_min = max(0, y - h)
    y1_max = min(y, m - h)
    
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        continue
        
    # For fixed w,h, minimize distance: (x1 + w/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + h/2 - y)^2
    # This is minimized when x1 + w/2 is close to x, y1 + h/2 is close to y
    # So optimal x1 is approximately x - w/2
    # Optimal y1 is approximately y - h/2
    
    # Check the boundaries and the ideal positions
    x1_candidates = []
    y1_candidates = []
    
    # Add boundary values
    x1_candidates.extend([x1_min, x1_max])
    y1_candidates.extend([y1_min, y1_max])
    
    # Add ideal values (if they fall in range)
    ideal_x1 = x - w / 2
    ideal_y1 = y - h / 2
    
    if x1_min <= ideal_x1 <= x1_max:
        x1_candidates.append(int(ideal_x1))
    if y1_min <= ideal_y1 <= y1_max:
        y1_candidates.append(int(ideal_y1))
        
    # Also add rounded values
    for val in [ideal_x1 - 0.5, ideal_x1 + 0.5]:
        if x1_min <= val <= x1_max:
            x1_candidates.append(int(val))
    for val in [ideal_y1 - 0.5, ideal_y1 + 0.5]:
        if y1_min <= val <= y1_max:
            y1_candidates.append(int(val))
    
    # Remove duplicates and filter valid ranges
    x1_candidates = [int(x1) for x1 in x1_candidates if x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max]
    y1_candidates = [int(y1) for y1 in y1_candidates if y1_min <= y1 <= y1_max]
    
    for x1 in x1_candidates:
        for y1 in y1_candidates:
            x2 = x1 + w
            y2 = y1 + h
            
            if x1 <= x <= x2 and y1 <= y <= y2 and x2 <= n and y2 <= m:
                center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
                center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
                dist_squared = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                
                if (dist_squared < best_dist_squared or 
                    (dist_squared == best_dist_squared and 
                     (x1, y1, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2))):
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                    best_dist_squared = dist_squared

print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

```python
best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
best_dist_squared = float('inf')
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output

Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the given point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (so (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b)
   - Is as close as possible to the center of the given point (x,y) in Euclidean distance
   - If multiple solutions, choose lexicographically minimum

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b means (x₂-x₁) = k×a and (y₂-y₁) = k×b for some positive integer k
2. We need 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ ≤ n and 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ ≤ m
3. Since we want the rectangle to be closest to (x,y), we want to minimize the distance from (x,y) to the center of the rectangle

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parameterize the problem
- Let the width be w = k×a and height be h = k×b
- Then x₁ = x - w/2 + offset, x₂ = x + w/2 + offset, etc.

Actually, let me think more systematically:

For a rectangle with width w and height h, centered at some point, the center of the rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2).

We want to minimize the Euclidean distance from (x,y) to this center.

### Step 2: Find valid width and height values
- Width w must be a multiple of a, so w = a×k for some k ≥ 1
- Height h must be a multiple of b, so h = b×k for the same k
- We need: x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ ≤ n, y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ ≤ m

### Step 3: For each valid k, find the best positioning
For a fixed k:
- w = a×k, h = b×k
- We need x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ = x₁ + w ≤ n, so x₁ ≤ n - w
- We need y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ = y₁ + h ≤ m, so y₁ ≤ m - h
- We need x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + w and y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + h

From x constraint: x - w ≤ x₁ ≤ x
From y constraint: y - h ≤ y₁ ≤ y

So x₁ ∈ [max(0, x - w), min(x, n - w)]
And y₁ ∈ [max(0, y - h), min(y, m - h)]

The center of the rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁ + x₁ + w)/2, (y₁ + y₁ + h)/2) = (x₁ + w/2, y₁ + h/2)

Distance to (x,y) squared is: (x₁ + w/2 - x)² + (y₁ + h/2 - y)²

We want to minimize this distance, then lexicographically minimize (x₁,y₁,x₂,y₂).

### Step 4: Implementation approach
1. Find maximum possible k
2. For each k from 1 to maximum, check if valid rectangle exists
3. For valid k, find the center that minimizes distance to (x,y)
4. Choose the k that gives minimum overall distance

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    best_rect = None
    best_distance = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible k values
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        w = a * k
        h = b * k
        
        # Determine valid range for x1 and y1
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        # If no valid positions for this k, skip
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For this k, we need to find x1, y1 that minimize distance
        # Center of rectangle is (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
        # Distance squared to (x,y): (x1 + w/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + h/2 - y)^2
        
        # The distance is minimized when x1 and y1 are chosen optimally
        # For fixed w,h, we want to minimize (x1 - (x - w/2))^2 + (y1 - (y - h/2))^2
        
        # Since we need x1 in [x1_min, x1_max] and y1 in [y1_min, y1_max],
        # we should choose x1 as close as possible to (x - w/2) within bounds
        # and similarly for y1
        
        x1_target = x - w / 2
        y1_target = y - h / 2
        
        x1 = int(round(x1_target))
        y1 = int(round(y1_target))
        
        # Adjust to make sure x1 is in valid range
        x1 = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, x1))
        y1 = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, y1))
        
        # If we need to choose integer, we should check both floor and ceil
        # Actually, since we want to minimize distance, we should pick the closest integer
        # to the target within bounds
        
        x1_options = []
        if x1_target >= x1_min and x1_target <= x1_max:
            x1_options.append(int(math.floor(x1_target)))
            x1_options.append(int(math.ceil(x1_target)))
        
        # Also include the bounds if needed
        x1_options.append(x1_min)
        x1_options.append(x1_max)
        
        # Remove duplicates and sort
        x1_options = list(set(x1_options))
        x1_options.sort()
        
        y1_options = []
        if y1_target >= y1_min and y1_target <= y1_max:
            y1_options.append(int(math.floor(y1_target)))
            y1_options.append(int(math.ceil(y1_target)))
        
        # Also include the bounds if needed
        y1_options.append(y1_min)
        y1_options.append(y1_max)
        
        y1_options = list(set(y1_options))
        y1_options.sort()
        
        # Try all combinations of x1, y1
        best_for_k = None
        min_dist_for_k = float('inf')
        
        for x1_choice in x1_options:
            if x1_choice < x1_min or x1_choice > x1_max:
                continue
                
            for y1_choice in y1_options:
                if y1_choice < y1_min or y1_choice > y1_max:
                    continue
                    
                x2 = x1_choice + w
                y2 = y1_choice + h
                
                # Verify rectangle contains (x,y)
                if not (x1_choice <= x <= x2 and y1_choice <= y <= y2):
                    continue
                    
                center_x = (x1_choice + x2) / 2
                center_y = (y1_choice + y2) / 2
                dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                
                if dist_sq < min_dist_for_k:
                    min_dist_for_k = dist_sq
                    best_for_k = (x1_choice, y1_choice, x2, y2)
                elif dist_sq == min_dist_for_k:
                    # Check lexicographic order
                    current_tuple = (x1_choice, y1_choice, x2, y2)
                    best_tuple = (best_for_k[0], best_for_k[1], best_for_k[2], best_for_k[3])
                    if current_tuple < best_tuple:
                        best_for_k = current_tuple
        
        if best_for_k is not None and min_dist_for_k < best_distance:
            best_distance = min_dist_for_k
            best_rect = best_for_k
    
    # If no solution found, try the maximum possible k (though it should always have a solution)
    if best_rect is None:
        # Fallback - try k = max_k with best possible center
        w = a * max_k
        h = b * max_k
        
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            x1_target = x - w / 2
            y1_target = y - h / 2
            
            x1 = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, int(round(x1_target))))
            y1 = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, int(round(y1_target))))
            
            x2 = x1 + w
            y2 = y1 + h
            
            best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
    
    print(best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])

solve()
```

Wait, I think there's a more efficient way. Let me reconsider.

Actually, looking at the examples more carefully:

For input "9 9 5 5 2 1", output is "1 3 9 7"
- Width = 9-1 = 8, Height = 7-3 = 4
- Ratio 8/4 = 2/1 ✓
- Contains (5,5)? 1≤5≤9 and 3≤5≤7 ✓
- Center is ((1+9)/2, (3+7)/2) = (5,5) - perfect!

This means the rectangle should be centered at (x,y) when possible.

Let me revise:

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

max_k = min(n // a, m // b)

best_rect = None
best_distance = float('inf')

# Try each possible k
for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    w = a * k
    h = b * k
    
    # Find valid x1 range: x1 <= x <= x1 + w and x1 >= 0, x1 + w <= n
    x1_min = max(0, x - w)
    x1_max = min(x, n - w)
    
    # Find valid y1 range: y1 <= y <= y1 + h and y1 >= 0, y1 + h <= m
    y1_min = max(0, y - h)
    y1_max = min(y, m - h)
    
    # If no valid positions, skip
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        continue
    
    # For optimal positioning, we want center at (x,y)
    # So x1 = x - w/2, y1 = y - h/2
    target_x1 = x - w / 2
    target_y1 = y - h / 2
    
    # Find integer x1 in valid range closest to target
    x1 = int(round(target_x1))
    if x1 < x1_min:
        x1 = x1_min
    elif x1 > x1_max:
        x1 = x1_max
    
    # Find integer y1 in valid range closest to target
    y1 = int(round(target_y1))
    if y1 < y1_min:
        y1 = y1_min
    elif y1 > y1_max:
        y1 = y1_max
    
    # Verify the rectangle contains (x,y)
    if not (x1 <= x <= x1 + w and y1 <= y <= y1 + h):
        continue
    
    x2 = x1 + w
    y2 = y1 + h
    
    # Calculate distance to center
    center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2
    center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2
    dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
    
    # Update best rectangle if better
    if best_rect is None or dist_sq < best_distance:
        best_distance = dist_sq
        best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
    elif dist_sq == best_distance:
        # Lexicographic comparison
        current = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
        best_current = (best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])
        if current < best_current:
            best_rect = current

print(best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])
```

    # Update best rectangle if better
    if best_rect is None or dist_sq < best_distance:
        best_distance = dist_sq
        best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
    elif dist_sq == best_distance:
        # Lexicographic comparison
        current = (x1, y1, x2, y2)
        best_current = (best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])
        if current < best_current:
            best_rect = current

print(best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
1. Contains the given point (x, y)
2. Has exact length-width ratio a/b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
3. Maximizes the area (or equivalently, has the largest possible dimensions)
4. If multiple solutions exist, choose the one closest to (x, y) in Euclidean distance
5. If still tied, choose lexicographically smallest

## Key Insights

1. For a rectangle with ratio a/b, if the width is w, then the length is (a/b) × w, which means w × a = length × b
2. So the dimensions must satisfy: (x₂-x₁) × b = (y₂-y₁) × a
3. This means (x₂-x₁) = k × a and (y₂-y₁) = k × b for some positive integer k
4. We want to maximize the rectangle, so we want to maximize k

## Constraints

The rectangle must contain (x, y), so:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂
- x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ ≤ n
- y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ ≤ m

For dimensions (k×a, k×b):
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k×b

## Finding Maximum k

For maximum k:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a → k×a ≥ x - x₁
- x₁ ≥ 0 → x₁ = max(0, x - k×a)
- x₂ = x₁ + k×a ≤ n → x₁ ≤ n - k×a
- Similarly for y: y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k×b → y₁ ≤ m - k×b
- y₁ = max(0, y - k×b)

The maximum possible k is constrained by:
- k×a ≤ n - x₁ ≤ n
- k×b ≤ m - y₁ ≤ m

To maximize k, we need to find the largest k such that:
- x₁ = max(0, x - k×a) ≥ 0
- x₂ = x₁ + k×a ≤ n
- y₁ = max(0, y - k×b) ≥ 0  
- y₂ = y₁ + k×b ≤ m

## Algorithm

1. Find the maximum valid k
2. For that k, find valid x₁, y₁
3. Check if multiple solutions exist and break ties by distance and lexicographic order

```python
def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Find maximum k such that we can have rectangle of dimensions (k*a, k*b)
    # and still contain (x, y)
    
    # The maximum possible k is limited by:
    # For x-direction: k*a ≤ n - x₁, where x₁ ≥ 0 and x₁ ≤ x
    # So k*a ≤ n - 0 = n, and k*a ≤ n - (x - k*a) = n - x + k*a
    # This gives us k*a ≤ n - x + k*a, which is always true
    
    # More concretely: we need x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k*a
    # So x₁ ≤ x and x₁ ≥ x - k*a
    # With x₁ ≥ 0, so we need max(0, x - k*a) ≤ min(x, n - k*a)
    
    # Simplifying: we need x - k*a ≤ n - k*a (always true for x ≤ n)
    # and x - k*a ≥ 0 when k*a ≤ x
    
    # The constraining factor is when we need to fit the entire rectangle
    # x₁ + k*a ≤ n and y₁ + k*b ≤ m
    
    # We need to find the maximum k such that:
    # There exists x₁ where:
    #   max(0, x - k*a) ≤ x₁ ≤ x
    #   x₁ + k*a ≤ n
    # So x₁ ≤ min(x, n - k*a)
    # And x₁ ≥ max(0, x - k*a)
    
    # This is possible when: max(0, x - k*a) ≤ min(x, n - k*a)
    
    # Since x - k*a ≤ x and x - k*a ≤ n - k*a when x ≤ n (which is true)
    # The constraint is: x - k*a ≤ n - k*a → x ≤ n (true)
    # and x - k*a ≥ 0 → k*a ≤ x (when x > 0)
    
    # Actually, the key is: we need x₁ > 0 and x₂ < n, and x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
    
    # Let's find maximum possible k
    max_k = 0
    
    # The maximum k is limited by the dimensions
    # For x-direction: k*a ≤ n (so k ≤ n/a)
    # For y-direction: k*b ≤ m (so k ≤ m/b)
    
    # But we also need to fit around point (x,y)
    # For any k, we need:
    #   x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k*a → x₁ ≥ x - k*a and x₁ ≤ x
    #   y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k*b → y₁ ≥ y - k*b and y₁ ≤ y
    
    # So we need: max(0, x - k*a) ≤ min(x, n - k*a) and max(0, y - k*b) ≤ min(y, m - k*b)
    
    # The limiting factor for k is min(n//a, m//b) but we also need to ensure we can place it around (x,y)
    
    # If k is too large, then x - k*a might be negative, so x₁ = 0
    # Then we need x ≤ k*a, so k ≥ x/a
    # Similarly, k ≥ y/b
    
    # For a given k, we can always place the rectangle if:
    # k*a ≥ x and k*b ≥ y  (when x₁=0, y₁=0)
    # OR 
    # k*a ≥ x - x₁ and k*b ≥ y - y₁ for some valid x₁, y₁
    
    # Actually, the maximum possible k is min(n//a, m//b)
    
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    # But we also need to ensure we can place it to contain (x,y)
    # For k > min(n//a, m//b), it's impossible
    
    # Check if k is actually feasible with our point constraints
    
    # For each k from max_k down to 0, check if it's feasible
    # But we want maximum k, so let's start with max_k and go down
    
    # Actually, we want to find the maximum k such that:
    # There exists x₁ in [max(0, x - k*a), min(x, n - k*a)]
    # There exists y₁ in [max(0, y - k*b), min(y, m - k*b)]
    
    # For maximum k, we just need to find the largest k ≤ min(n//a, m//b)
    # that satisfies the bounds above
    
    # But since k is maximized by min(n//a, m//b), and we want maximum,
    # we start with k = min(n//a, m//b) and go down until feasible
    
    # However, let's think about it differently: for a given k, can we place the rectangle?
    # We need:
    # x₁ in [max(0, x - k*a), x] ∩ [0, n - k*a]
    # y₁ in [max(0, y - k*b), y] ∩ [0, m - k*b]
    
    # The intersection is non-empty if:
    # max(0, x - k*a) ≤ min(x, n - k*a)
    # and max(0, y - k*b) ≤ min(y, m - k*b)
    
    # Since x ≤ n and y ≤ m, and k*a ≤ n, k*b ≤ m, the second condition is equivalent to:
    # max(0, x - k*a) ≤ n - k*a (which is true since x - k*a ≤ n - k*a)
    # and max(0, y - k*b) ≤ m - k*b (which is true since y - k*b ≤ m - k*b)
    
    # So the only constraint is that max(0, x - k*a) ≤ min(x, n - k*a)
    # which simplifies to:
    # if x - k*a ≤ 0, then 0 ≤ min(x, n - k*a) → which is true
    # if x - k*a > 0, then x - k*a ≤ n - k*a → x ≤ n → true
    
    # Actually, max(0, x - k*a) ≤ min(x, n - k*a) is always true!
    
    # This means any k ≤ min(n//a, m//b) is feasible!
    
    # So maximum k is min(n//a, m//b)
    
    k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    # But wait - we need to ensure we can actually contain (x,y)
    # For the rectangle to contain (x,y), we need:
    # x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k*a and y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k*b
    
    # We can choose x₁ in [max(0, x - k*a), x] and x₁ ≤ n - k*a
    # So we need max(0, x - k*a) ≤ min(x, n - k*a)
    
    # As above, this is always true because:
    # If x - k*a ≤ 0, then max(0, x - k*a) = 0, and min(x, n - k*a) ≥ 0
    # If x - k*a > 0, then we need x - k*a ≤ n - k*a → x ≤ n → true
    
    # Similarly for y
    
    # So for k = min(n//a, m//b), we can always find such a rectangle
    
    # Now, for this k, we need to find x₁, y₁ to minimize Euclidean distance from (x,y) to center
    # Center is at ((x₁ + x₂)/2, (y₁ + y₂)/2) = ((x₁ + x₁ + k*a)/2, (y₁ + y₁ + k*b)/2) = (x₁ + k*a/2, y₁ + k*b/2)
    
    # Distance squared: (x - (x₁ + k*a/2))² + (y - (y₁ + k*b/2))²
    
    # We want to minimize this, then lexicographically smallest if tied.
    
    # So we need to find x₁ in [max(0, x - k*a), min(x, n - k*a)] 
    # and y₁ in [max(0, y - k*b), min(y, m - k*b)]
    
    # For distance minimization, we want:
    # x₁ ≈ x - k*a/2
    # y₁ ≈ y - k*b/2
    
    # But x₁ must be in valid range
    
    x1_min = max(0, x - k * a)
    x1_max = min(x, n - k * a)
    y1_min = max(0, y - k * b)
    y1_max = min(y, m - k * b)
    
    # If the ranges are invalid, we need to handle it
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        # This shouldn't happen given our constraints
        k = 0
        x1_min = 0
        x1_max = 0
        y1_min = 0
        y1_max = 0
    
    # Find best x1 and y1
    best_x1 = x1_min
    best_y1 = y1_min
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Try to find optimal values
    # For distance, we want x1 ≈ x - k*a/2 and y1 ≈ y - k*b/2
    
    target_x1 = x - k * a / 2
    target_y1 = y - k * b / 2
    
    # Check bounds
    x1_candidate = int(round(target_x1))
    y1_candidate = int(round(target_y1))
    
    # But we need to check if within bounds
    x1_candidate = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, x1_candidate))
    y1_candidate = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, y1_candidate))
    
    # Check if other nearby candidates also give same distance
    candidates = []
    
    # Check the interval around target_x1 and target_y1
    # Since we want to minimize distance, and distance is quadratic, 
    # the optimal is at the target or boundary
    
    # Add nearby integers
    for dx in range(-1, 2):
        for dy in range(-1, 2):
            cand_x1 = x1_min + dx
            cand_y1 = y1_min + dy
            
            if cand_x1 < x1_min or cand_x1 > x1_max:
                continue
            if cand_y1 < y1_min or cand_y1 > y1_max:
                continue
                
            x2 = cand_x1 + k * a
            y2 = cand_y1 + k * b
            
            if x2 > n or y2 > m:
                continue
                
            # Calculate distance to center
            cx = (cand_x1 + x2) / 2
            cy = (cand_y1 + y2) / 2
            dist_sq = (x - cx) ** 2 + (y - cy) ** 2
            
            candidates.append((dist_sq, cand_x1, cand_y1))
    
    # Add the calculated targets (without the 1-2 range) to ensure we have enough
    # But let's be systematic
    
    # Actually, since we have continuous optimization, the minimum of distance occurs
    # when x1 is as close as possible to x - k*a/2, and y1 to y - k*b/2
    
    # So search in the range around the target
    best_found = False
    
    # First try the ideal values
    x1_try = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, int(target_x1)))
    y1_try = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, int(target_y1)))
    
    # Also check floor and ceil
    for x1_val in [int(target_x1), int(target_x1) + 1, int(target_x1) - 1]:
        for y1_val in [int(target_y1), int(target_y1) + 1, int(target_y1) - 1]:
            if x1_val < x1_min or x1_val > x1_max or y1_val < y1_min or y1_val > y1_max:
                continue
                
            x2 = x1_val + k * a
            y2 = y1_val + k * b
            
            if x2 > n or y2 > m:
                continue
                
            cx = (x1_val + x2) / 2
            cy = (y1_val + y2) / 2
            dist_sq = (x - cx) ** 2 + (y - cy) ** 2
            
            if not best_found or dist_sq < best_dist_sq or (dist_sq == best_dist_sq and (x1_val < best_x1 or (x1_val == best_x1 and y1_val < best_y1))):
                best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                best_x1 = x1_val
                best_y1 = y1_val
                best_found = True
    
    # If no candidates found (shouldn't happen), use minimum
    if not best_found:
        best_x1 = x1_min
        best_y1 = y1_min
    
    x2 = best_x1 + k * a
    y2 = best_y1 + k * b
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, x2, y2)
```

Wait, I think my approach is overcomplicated. Let me simplify and reconsider.

## Refined Approach

For the maximum rectangle with ratio a:b, the dimensions are k*a by k*b for some integer k.

The maximum possible k is min(n//a, m//b).

For this k, we want to place the rectangle to contain (x,y) and minimize distance to center.

The center is at (x1 + k*a/2, y1 + k*b/2).

We want this center to be as close as possible to (x,y).

So we want x1 + k*a/2 ≈ x and y1 + k*b/2 ≈ y.

Thus x1 ≈ x - k*a/2 and y1 ≈ y - k*b/2.

We need to find valid x1, y1 in their bounded ranges, and pick the one that minimizes distance.

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Maximum possible k such that k*a <= n and k*b <= m
k = min(n // a, m // b)

# Bounds for x1 and y1
x1_min = max(0, x - k * a)
x1_max = min(x, n - k * a)
y1_min = max(0, y - k * b)
y1_max = min(y, m - k * b)

# If no valid position exists, use degenerate case
if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
    # This shouldn't happen with our constraints
    k = 0
    x1_min = 0
    x1_max = 0
    y1_min = 0
    y1_max = 0

# Find the x1, y1 that minimize distance to (x,y)
# Center of rectangle: (x1 + k*a/2, y1 + k*b/2)
best_x1 = x1_min
best_y1 = y1_min
best_dist_sq = float('inf')

# Target values for center
target_x_center = x
target_y_center = y

# Try values around the ideal positions
ideal_x1 = x - k * a / 2
ideal_y1 = y - k * b / 2

# Check nearby integer values
candidates = []

# Add the ideal values and their neighbors
for dx in range(-1, 2):
    for dy in range(-1, 2):
        x1_candidate = int(ideal_x1) + dx
        y1_candidate = int(ideal_y1) + dy
        
        # Check if within bounds
        if x1_candidate < x1_min or x1_candidate > x1_max:
            continue
        if y1_candidate < y1_min or y1_candidate > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # Check if rectangle fits in bounds
        x2 = x1_candidate + k * a
        y2 = y1_candidate + k * b
        
        if x2 > n or y2 > m:
            continue
            
        # Calculate distance to center
        center_x = (x1_candidate + x2) / 2
        center_y = (y1_candidate + y2) / 2
        dist_sq = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
        
        candidates.append((dist_sq, x1_candidate, y1_candidate))

# If no candidates, use minimum
if not candidates:
    best_x1 = x1_min
    best_y1 = y1_min
else:
    # Sort by distance, then lexicographically
    candidates.sort(key=lambda item: (item[0], item[1], item[2]))
    best_dist_sq, best_x1, best_y1 = candidates[0]

x2 = best_x1 + k * a
y2 = best_y1 + k * b

print(best_x1, best_y1, x2, y2)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates from (0,n) × (0,m)
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
   - Is as close as possible to the center of the grid (minimizing Euclidean distance from center)
   - If multiple solutions, choose lexicographically smallest

## Mathematical Analysis

Given the ratio condition: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b

This means: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)

We can express this as: x₂-x₁ = (a/b)(y₂-y₁)

Since we need integer coordinates, we need to ensure the ratio holds exactly.

Let's say the width (y-direction) is w = y₂-y₁, then the length (x-direction) is (a/b)w.

For this to be integer, w must be a multiple of b. So let w = b·k for some integer k ≥ 0.

Then length = (a/b)·b·k = a·k.

So we have:
- width = b·k
- length = a·k

The sub-rectangle has dimensions a·k × b·k.

## Constraints

The rectangle must contain (x,y), so:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂

We need to find valid ranges for x₁, x₂, y₁, y₂.

## Bounds Analysis

For a given k:
- x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ ≤ n, so 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ ≤ n
- y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ ≤ m, so 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ ≤ m

For fixed dimensions a·k × b·k:
- x₁ can range from max(0, x - a·k) to min(x, n - a·k)
- x₂ = x₁ + a·k, so x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ = x₁ + a·k, which means x₁ ≥ x - a·k and x₁ ≤ x
- y₁ can range from max(0, y - b·k) to min(y, m - b·k)
- y₂ = y₁ + b·k, so y₁ ≥ y - b·k and y₁ ≤ y

Actually, let me reconsider the constraints more carefully.

For the rectangle to contain (x,y):
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + a·k
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + b·k

So:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + a·k → x - a·k ≤ x₁ ≤ x
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + b·k → y - b·k ≤ y₁ ≤ y

Also:
- x₁ ≥ 0, x₁ + a·k ≤ n → x₁ ≤ n - a·k
- y₁ ≥ 0, y₁ + b·k ≤ m → y₁ ≤ m - b·k

## Solution Strategy

1. Find valid values of k (the scaling factor)
2. For each valid k, find valid (x₁, y₁) pairs
3. Among all valid rectangles, choose one that minimizes Euclidean distance from (x,y)
4. If tie, choose lexicographically smallest

The valid range for k is determined by the constraints:
- a·k ≤ n (for x-direction)
- b·k ≤ m (for y-direction)
- x - a·k ≥ 0 (to have x₁ ≥ 0)
- y - b·k ≥ 0 (to have y₁ ≥ 0)
- x ≤ x + a·k ≤ n (so a·k ≤ n-x)
- y ≤ y + b·k ≤ m (so b·k ≤ m-y)

Actually, the constraints are:
- a·k ≤ n (since we need a·k width in x-direction)
- b·k ≤ m (since we need b·k width in y-direction)
- x ≥ a·k (from x₁ ≥ 0 and x₁ ≤ x)
- x ≥ x - a·k (this is always true)
- y ≥ b·k

Wait, let me be more precise:

From x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + a·k:
- x ≥ x₁
- x ≤ x₁ + a·k → x₁ ≥ x - a·k

From y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + b·k:
- y ≥ y₁
- y ≤ y₁ + b·k → y₁ ≥ y - b·k

Also:
- x₁ ≥ 0 → x ≥ x - a·k
- x₁ + a·k ≤ n → x ≤ x + a·k ≤ n → a·k ≤ n-x
- y₁ ≥ 0 → y ≥ y - b·k  
- y₁ + b·k ≤ m → y ≤ y + b·k ≤ m → b·k ≤ m-y

So valid k must satisfy:
- a·k ≤ n (basic constraint)
- b·k ≤ m (basic constraint)
- a·k ≤ n - x (since x₁ + a·k ≤ n and x₁ ≥ 0)
- b·k ≤ m - y (since y₁ + b·k ≤ m and y₁ ≥ 0)
- x - a·k ≥ 0 (so x₁ ≥ 0)
- y - b·k ≥ 0 (so y₁ ≥ 0)

Actually, let me simplify the range:
- k ≤ n/a (from a·k ≤ n)
- k ≤ m/b (from b·k ≤ m)
- k ≤ (n-x)/a (from a·k ≤ n-x)
- k ≤ (m-y)/b (from b·k ≤ m-y)
- k ≤ x/a (from x - a·k ≥ 0)
- k ≤ y/b (from y - b·k ≥ 0)

The most restrictive upper bound is k ≤ min(n/a, m/b, (n-x)/a, (m-y)/b, x/a, y/b)

And k ≥ 0.

So k_max = min(n//a, m//b, (n-x)//a, (m-y)//b, x//a, y//b)

But since we work with integers, k can be from 0 to k_max.

## Step-by-Step Algorithm

1. Calculate the maximum valid k: k_max = min(n//a, m//b, (n-x)//a, (m-y)//b, x//a, y//b)
2. For each k from 0 to k_max:
   - Calculate width = b*k, length = a*k
   - Calculate valid x₁ range: max(0, x - a*k) to min(x, n - a*k)
   - Calculate valid y₁ range: max(0, y - b*k) to min(y, m - b*k)
   - For each valid (x₁, y₁), calculate the center of rectangle: ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) where x₂ = x₁ + a*k, y₂ = y₁ + b*k
   - Calculate Euclidean distance from (x,y) to center
3. Among all valid rectangles, choose the one with minimum distance, then lexicographically smallest.

However, we need to be careful - we might not need to check all k values. The maximum possible k is bounded.

Let me implement this:

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find maximum valid k
k_max = min(n // a, m // b, (n - x) // a, (m - y) // b, x // a, y // b)

best_distance_squared = float('inf')
best_rect = None

# Try all possible values of k from 0 to k_max
for k in range(k_max + 1):
    width = b * k
    length = a * k
    
    # Valid ranges for x1 and y1
    x1_low = max(0, x - length)  # x1 >= x - length
    x1_high = min(x, n - length)  # x1 <= x and x1 + length <= n
    
    y1_low = max(0, y - width)  # y1 >= y - width
    y1_high = min(y, m - width)  # y1 <= y and y1 + width <= m
    
    # If ranges are valid
    if x1_low <= x1_high and y1_low <= y1_high:
        # Try all valid (x1, y1) pairs
        for x1 in range(x1_low, x1_high + 1):
            x2 = x1 + length
            for y1 in range(y1_low, y1_high + 1):
                y2 = y1 + width
                
                # Calculate center of rectangle
                center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
                center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
                
                # Calculate Euclidean distance from (x,y) to center
                distance_squared = (x - center_x)**2 + (y - center_y)**2
                
                # Check if this is better
                if (distance_squared < best_distance_squared or 
                    (distance_squared == best_distance_squared and 
                     (best_rect is None or 
                      (x1, y1, x2, y2) < best_rect))):
                    best_distance_squared = distance_squared
                    best_rect = (x1, y1, x2, y2)

print(best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])
```

Wait, this has a problem: the time complexity could be O(k_max * (range_x1) * (range_y1)) which might be too high for large grids.

I need to optimize. Since we only care about minimizing distance, and we want lexicographically smallest when tied, I should think about when the distance is minimized.

Actually, for a fixed k, the center of the rectangle is at ((x1+x2)/2, (y1+y2)/2) = ((x1 + x1 + a*k)/2, (y1 + y1 + b*k)/2) = (x1 + a*k/2, y1 + b*k/2).

The distance to (x,y) is:
(x - (x1 + a*k/2))² + (y - (y1 + b*k/2))²

The minimum distance occurs when x1 is as close as possible to (x - a*k/2) and similarly for y1.

So for each k, I should:
- Find the optimal x1 in [x1_low, x1_high] that minimizes |x - (x1 + a*k/2)|
- Find the optimal y1 in [y1_low, y1_high] that minimizes |y - (y1 + b*k/2)|
- Then the distance is minimized

The optimal x1 is the value closest to (x - a*k/2) in the valid range.

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find maximum valid k
k_max = min(n // a, m // b, (n - x) // a, (m - y) // b, x // a, y // b)

best_distance_squared = float('inf')
best_rect = None

# Try all possible values of k from 0 to k_max
for k in range(k_max + 1):
    length = a * k
    width = b * k
    
    # Valid ranges for x1 and y1
    x1_low = max(0, x - length)
    x1_high = min(x, n - length)
    
    y1_low = max(0, y - width)
    y1_high = min(y, m - width)
    
    # If ranges are valid
    if x1_low <= x1_high and y1_low <= y1_high:
        # Find optimal x1 to minimize distance to x
        target_x = x - length / 2.0
        if target_x < x1_low:
            x1_opt = x1_low
        elif target_x > x1_high:
            x1_opt = x1_high
        else:
            x1_opt = round(target_x)
            # Clamp to valid range
            if x1_opt < x1_low:
                x1_opt = x1_low
            elif x1_opt > x1_high:
                x1_opt = x1_high
        
        # Find optimal y1 to minimize distance to y
        target_y = y - width / 2.0
        if target_y < y1_low:
            y1_opt = y1_low
        elif target_y > y1_high:
            y1_opt = y1_high
        else:
            y1_opt = round(target_y)
            # Clamp to valid range
            if y1_opt < y1_low:
                y1_opt = y1_low
            elif y1_opt > y1_high:
                y1_opt = y1_high
        
        # But we need to be careful about rounding - we want the closest value
        # For simplicity, let's just use the closest value within bounds
        
        # Actually, since we're dealing with integers, we want the integer x1 that minimizes |x - (x1 + length/2)|
        # This happens when x1 is as close as possible to (x - length/2)
        
        # Get the actual optimal x1
        x1_actual = x1_low
        if x1_low <= x1_high:
            x1_candidate = int(x - length / 2)
            if x1_candidate < x1_low:
                x1_actual = x1_low
            elif x1_candidate > x1_high:
                x1_actual = x1_high
            else:
                # Check both candidates: x1_candidate and x1_candidate+1, etc. 
                # Since we're at integer bounds, just take the closest
                if abs(x - (x1_candidate + length/2)) < abs(x - (x1_candidate + 1 + length/2)):
                    x1_actual = x1_candidate
                else:
                    x1_actual = x1_candidate + 1
                # Clamp to bounds
                if x1_actual < x1_low:
                    x1_actual = x1_low
                elif x1_actual > x1_high:
                    x1_actual = x1_high
        
        # Actually, much simpler: just try the closest valid integers
        x1_candidate = int(x - length / 2)
        x1_actual = x1_low
        if x1_low <= x1_candidate <= x1_high:
            x1_actual = x1_candidate
        elif x1_candidate < x1_low:
            x1_actual = x1_low
        else:  # x_candidate > x1_high
            x1_actual = x1_high
            
        # Similarly for y1
        y1_candidate = int(y - width / 2)
        y1_actual = y1_low
        if y1_low <= y1_candidate <= y1_high:
            y1_actual = y1_candidate
        elif y1_candidate < y1_low:
            y1_actual = y1_low
        else:  # y1_candidate > y1_high
            y1_actual = y1_high
            
        x2 = x1_actual + length
        y2 = y1_actual + width
        
        # Calculate distance to center
        center_x = (x1_actual + x2) / 2.0
        center_y = (y1_actual + y2) / 2.0
        distance_squared = (x - center_x)**2 + (y - center_y)**2
        
        # Check if this is better
        if (distance_squared < best_distance_squared or 
            (distance_squared == best_distance_squared and 
             (best_rect is None or (x1_actual, y1_actual, x2, y2) < best_rect))):
            best_distance_squared = distance_squared
            best_rect = (x1_actual, y1_actual, x2, y2)

print(best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])
```

This is getting complex. Let me simplify and implement a cleaner version:

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find maximum valid k
k_max = min(n // a, m // b, (n - x) // a, (m - y) // b, x // a, y // b)

best_distance_squared = float('inf')
best_rect = None

for k in range(k_max + 1):
    length = a * k
    width = b * k
    
    # Valid x1 range
    x1_low = max(0, x - length)
    x1_high = min(x, n - length)
    
    # Valid y1 range
    y1_low = max(0, y - width)
    y1_high = min(y, m - width)
    
    if x1_low > x1_high or y1_low > y1_high:
        continue
    
    # Find x1 that minimizes distance
    # We want to minimize |x - (x1 + length/2)|
    target_x = x - length / 2.0
    x1_val = x1_low
    if target_x >= x1_low and target_x <= x1_high:
        x1_val = int(target_x)
        if x1_val < x1_low:
            x1_val = x1_low
        elif x1_val > x1_high:
            x1_val = x1_high
    else:
        if target_x < x1_low:
            x1_val = x1_low
        else:
            x1_val = x1_high
    
    # Find y1 that minimizes distance
    target_y = y - width / 2.0
    y1_val = y1_low
    if target_y >= y1_low and target_y <= y1_high:
        y1_val = int(target_y)
        if y1_val < y1_low:
            y1_val = y1_low
        elif y1_val > y1_high:
            y1_val = y1_high
    else:
        if target_y < y1_low:
            y1_val = y1_low
        else:
            y1_val = y1_high
    
    # Ensure we're in valid bounds
    x1_val = max(x1_low, min(x1_val, x1_high))
    y1_val = max(y1_low, min(y1_val, y1_high))
    
    x2 = x1_val + length
    y2 = y1_val + width
    
    # Calculate Euclidean distance from center to (x,y)
    center_x = (x1_val + x2) / 2.0
    center_y = (y1_val + y2) / 2.0
    distance_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
    
    # Update best solution
    if (distance_squared < best_distance_squared or 
        (distance_squared == best_distance_squared and 
         (best_rect is None or (x1_val, y1_val, x2, y2) < best_rect))):
        best_distance_squared = distance_squared
        best_rect = (x1_val, y1_val, x2, y2)

print(best_rect[0], best_rect[1], best_rect[2], best_rect[3])
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find a sub-rectangle in an n×m grid that:
1. Contains the point (x, y)
2. Has exactly ratio (a/b) between its width and height
3. Maximizes the area (or finds the maximum possible rectangle)
4. If multiple solutions exist, prefers the one closest to (x, y) in Euclidean distance
5. If still tied, prefers lexicographically smallest solution

## Key Insights

1. The rectangle must satisfy: (x₂ - x₁) / (y₂ - y₁) = a/b
2. This means: b(x₂ - x₁) = a(y₂ - y₁)
3. We can express width = w, height = h, where w/h = a/b, so w = (a/b)h
4. Since w and h must be integers, h must be divisible by b, and w must be divisible by a
5. Let h = k × b, then w = k × a for some positive integer k

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Find valid dimensions**: For each possible k, we get width = k×a, height = k×b
2. **Check bounds**: The rectangle must fit within [0,n]×[0,m] and contain (x,y)
3. **Find valid positions**: For each valid (w,h), find valid (x₁,y₁) such that:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + w ≤ n
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + h ≤ m
4. **Maximize area**: Larger k gives larger area, so we want maximum possible k
5. **Closest to (x,y)**: Among valid solutions, find the one with center closest to (x,y)
6. **Lexicographic order**: If still tied, choose lexicographically smallest

## Implementation

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Find the maximum possible k such that:
    # width = k*a <= n, height = k*b <= m
    # and the rectangle can contain (x,y)
    
    # Maximum possible k considering width and height constraints
    max_k_width = n // a if a > 0 else float('inf')
    max_k_height = m // b if b > 0 else float('inf')
    
    max_k = min(max_k_width, max_k_height)
    
    # If no valid k, return impossible (but problem guarantees solution)
    if max_k == 0:
        # Try to find any valid rectangle
        # For small k, we might have solutions
        max_k = 1
    
    # We want maximum k, so start with max_k and work down
    best_solution = None
    
    # Try all possible k from max_k down to 1
    for k in range(max_k, 0, -1):
        width = k * a
        height = k * b
        
        # Check if rectangle can contain (x,y)
        # x1 <= x <= x1 + width <= n
        # y1 <= y <= y1 + height <= m
        
        # x1 can range from: max(0, x - width) to min(x, n - width)
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)
        
        # y1 can range from: max(0, y - height) to min(y, m - height)
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)
        
        # If valid ranges exist
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            # Find the center of this rectangle
            center_x = (x1_min + x1_max) / 2.0 + width / 2.0
            center_y = (y1_min + y1_max) / 2.0 + height / 2.0
            
            # Try all valid combinations of (x1, y1)
            # In practice, we just need to find one that minimizes distance
            # and then break to get the maximum k solution
            x1_candidate = x1_min
            y1_candidate = y1_min
            
            # We need to find the closest to (x,y)
            # The center of rectangle is ((x1 + x1 + width)/2, (y1 + y1 + height)/2)
            # = (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
            
            # We want to minimize distance to (x,y)
            # Distance squared = (x1 + width/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)^2
            
            # Since we're maximizing k, we just need one valid solution with max k
            # For lexicographic order, we try to minimize x1 first, then y1
            
            # First try x1 = x1_min, y1 = y1_min
            center_x1 = x1_candidate + width / 2.0
            center_y1 = y1_candidate + height / 2.0
            
            # Check if this is maximum k solution
            current_best = (x1_candidate, y1_candidate, x1_candidate + width, y1_candidate + height)
            
            # Actually, since we're iterating from max_k down, and we want maximum k,
            # we just need to find the best (x1,y1) for this k that minimizes distance
            # and is lexicographic minimum if tied
            
            # To be systematic, we should check if there's a better (x1,y1) in current k
            best_center_x = center_x1
            best_center_y = center_y1
            best_x1 = x1_candidate
            best_y1 = y1_candidate
            
            # Try all possible (x1,y1) in the valid range to find minimum distance
            # But the ranges might be too large, so we need to be smarter
            
            # Actually, since the distance function is convex, the minimum is achieved
            # when x1 and y1 are as close as possible to the optimal values
            
            # For lexicographic minimum, we start with minimum x1 and y1
            
            # But we need the one closest to (x,y) first
            
            # Let's find the values that minimize distance from (x,y)
            # For x1: we want to minimize |(x1 + width/2) - x|
            # So x1 should be as close as possible to x - width/2
            
            opt_x1 = x - width / 2.0
            x1_val = int(math.floor(opt_x1))
            
            # But x1 must be in [x1_min, x1_max]
            x1_val = max(x1_min, min(x1_val, x1_max))
            
            # For y1: minimize |(y1 + height/2) - y|
            opt_y1 = y - height / 2.0
            y1_val = int(math.floor(opt_y1))
            
            y1_val = max(y1_min, min(y1_val, y1_max))
            
            # Now we have a candidate that minimizes distance
            # But we should also check if there are other candidates in the valid range
            # For simplicity, since we want maximum k, we just find the lexicographically smallest
            # valid rectangle with maximum k
            
            # Let's find the lexicographically smallest valid rectangle with maximum k
            current_x1 = x1_min
            current_y1 = y1_min
            
            # Check if we can improve based on distance
            # Actually, we want the closest to (x,y), so we should find the one that minimizes distance
            # The optimal x1 is x - width/2, optimal y1 is y - height/2
            
            best_x1 = x1_min
            best_y1 = y1_min
            
            # Optimal x1 for minimum distance
            if width > 0:
                best_x1 = int(x - width / 2.0)
                best_x1 = max(x1_min, min(best_x1, x1_max))
            else:
                best_x1 = x1_min
            
            # Optimal y1 for minimum distance
            if height > 0:
                best_y1 = int(y - height / 2.0)
                best_y1 = max(y1_min, min(best_y1, y1_max))
            else:
                best_y1 = y1_min
            
            # But we need to ensure integer values
            best_x1 = int(round(best_x1))
            best_y1 = int(round(best_y1))
            
            # Final check bounds
            best_x1 = max(x1_min, min(best_x1, x1_max))
            best_y1 = max(y1_min, min(best_y1, y1_max))
            
            # We have a candidate for maximum k
            best_solution = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x1 + width, best_y1 + height)
            
            # Since we're going from max_k down, and we want maximum k,
            # we break at the first valid k and choose the best within that k
            break
    
    # If no solution found with max_k, try smaller k
    if best_solution is None:
        # Try k = 1
        width = a
        height = b
        
        x1_min = max(0, x - width)
        x1_max = min(x, n - width)
        
        y1_min = max(0, y - height)
        y1_max = min(y, m - height)

            best_y1 = y1_min
            best_solution = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x1 + width, best_y1 + height)
```

```python
# Find maximum possible k such that k*a <= n and k*b <= m
max_k = min(n // a, m // b) if a > 0 and b > 0 else 0

    max_k = 1

# For maximum k, find the best (x1, y1)

height = max_k * b

# x1 must satisfy: x1 <= x <= x1 + width and x1 >= 0 and x1 + width <= n
x1_min = max(0, x - width)
x1_max = min(x, n - width)

y1_max = min(y, m - height)

    width = a
    height = b
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)

# Find the optimal (x1, y1) that minimizes distance to (x,y)
# Distance squared: (x1 + width/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + height/2 - y)^2
# This is minimized when x1 is closest to x - width/2, y1 is closest to y - height/2

opt_x1 = x - width / 2.0
opt_y1 = y - height / 2.0

# Apply bounds
best_x1 = int(round(opt_x1))
best_x1 = max(x1_min, min(best_x1, x1_max))

best_y1 = int(round(opt_y1))
best_y1 = max(y1_min, min(best_y1, y1_max))

# If there are ties, we need lexicographically smallest
# But since we're minimizing distance, and if multiple have same distance,
# we need to choose lexicographically smallest

# Actually, let's try the boundaries first to ensure we get valid solution
x1_candidates = [x1_min, x1_max]
if x1_min < x1_max:
    x1_candidates.append(int(x - width / 2.0))

y1_candidates = [y1_min, y1_max]
if y1_min < y1_max:
    y1_candidates.append(int(y - height / 2.0))

# Check all candidates and find best
best_center_dist_sq = float('inf')
best_x1, best_y1 = x1_min, y1_min
best_x2, best_y2 = x1_min + width, y1_min + height

for x1 in x1_candidates:
    for y1 in y1_candidates:
        if x1 < x1_min or x1 > x1_max or y1 < y1_min or y1 > y1_max:
            continue
            
        x2 = x1 + width
        y2 = y1 + height
        
        if x2 > n or y2 > m:
            continue
            
        # Check if contains (x,y)
        if x1 <= x <= x2 and y1 <= y <= y2:
            center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
            center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
            dist_sq = (center_x - x)**2 + (center_y - y)**2
            
            if dist_sq < best_center_dist_sq:
                best_center_dist_sq = dist_sq
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
            elif dist_sq == best_center_dist_sq:
                # Lexicographic order: prefer smaller x1, then smaller y1
                if (x1 < best_x1) or (x1 == best_x1 and y1 < best_y1):
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2

print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find maximum k such that k*a <= n and k*b <= m
max_k = min(n // a, m // b) if a > 0 and b > 0 else 0

# For maximum k, calculate dimensions
width = max_k * a
height = max_k * b

# If no valid k, use k=1
if max_k == 0:
    max_k = 1
    width = a
    height = b

# Find valid ranges for x1 and y1
x1_min = max(0, x - width)
x1_max = min(x, n - width)
y1_min = max(0, y - height)
y1_max = min(y, m - height)

# If no valid range, return error (shouldn't happen with valid input)
if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
    # This shouldn't happen with valid input, but just in case
    x1_min, x1_max = 0, n
    y1_min, y1_max = 0, m
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)

# Find optimal x1 and y1 that minimizes distance to (x,y)

best_y1 = int(round(opt_y1))

best_y1 = max(y1_min, min(best_y1, y1_max))

y2 = best_y1 + height
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

100 100 52 50 46 56

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b
   - Minimizes Euclidean distance from center to (x,y)
   - If tie, chooses lexicographically smallest (x1,y1,x2,y2)

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition: (x₂ - x₁)/(y₂ - y₁) = a/b
2. This means: b(x₂ - x₁) = a(y₂ - y₁)
3. We can express the width and height in terms of a parameter k: width = a*k, height = b*k for some positive integer k
4. The rectangle must contain (x,y), so:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂
   - x₂ - x₁ = a*k
   - y₂ - y₁ = b*k

## Solution Steps

1. For a given k, we have:
   - width = a*k
   - height = b*k
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + a*k
   - y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + b*k

2. Constraints give us:
   - x₁ ≤ x ≤ x + a*k (so x₁ ≤ x)
   - x₁ + a*k ≥ x (so x₁ ≥ x - a*k)
   - So: x - a*k ≤ x₁ ≤ x
   - Similarly: y - b*k ≤ y₁ ≤ y

3. For valid x₁, we need: x - a*k ≥ 0 (so x₁ ≥ 0)
   For valid y₁, we need: y - b*k ≥ 0 (so y₁ ≥ 0)

4. Also, x₁ + a*k ≤ n (so x₁ ≤ n - a*k)
   and y₁ + b*k ≤ m (so y₁ ≤ m - b*k)

5. So for valid k:
   - x - a*k ≥ 0 → k ≤ x/a
   - x₁ ≤ x → x₁ ≤ x
   - x₁ ≥ x - a*k → x - a*k ≤ x  
   - x₁ ≤ n - a*k → x ≤ n - a*k → k ≤ (n-x)/a
   - Similarly for y: k ≤ (m-y)/b

6. So k must satisfy: k ≤ min(x/a, (n-x)/a, (m-y)/b)

7. We want to maximize the rectangle area (or make it larger), but the problem is asking for the rectangle closest to (x,y) in terms of center distance.

## Center Distance Analysis

The center of the rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁+x₁+a*k)/2, (y₁+y₁+b*k)/2) = (x₁ + a*k/2, y₁ + b*k/2)

Distance squared from (x,y): (x - (x₁ + a*k/2))² + (y - (y₁ + b*k/2))²

We want to minimize this, subject to constraints.

## Algorithm

1. Find the maximum possible k that satisfies all constraints
2. For that k, find valid x₁ and y₁ that minimize center distance
3. Among solutions, pick lexicographically smallest

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Find maximum valid k
    # k must satisfy:
    # x - a*k >= 0 -> k <= x/a
    # n - x - a*k >= 0 -> k <= (n-x)/a  
    # m - y - b*k >= 0 -> k <= (m-y)/b
    # k >= 0
    
    max_k = min(x // a, (n - x) // a, (m - y) // b)
    
    if max_k <= 0:
        # No valid rectangle
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    # For k = max_k, we want to minimize distance from center to (x,y)
    # Center is at (x1 + a*k/2, y1 + b*k/2)
    # Distance squared: (x - (x1 + a*k/2))² + (y - (y1 + b*k/2))²
    
    # We have constraints:
    # x1 >= max(0, x - a*k)
    # x1 <= min(n - a*k, x)  (since x1 <= x)
    # Actually: x1 >= x - a*k and x1 <= x
    # and x1 >= 0, x1 <= n - a*k
    
    x1_min = max(0, x - a * max_k)
    x1_max = min(x, n - a * max_k)
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - b * max_k)
    y1_max = min(y, m - b * max_k)
    
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        # This shouldn't happen if our k is valid
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    # Find x1, y1 that minimize distance to (x,y)
    best_x1 = x1_min
    best_y1 = y1_min
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Try all possible x1 and y1 in the range
    # Since we want lexicographically smallest when distance is same,
    # we'll check in order of x1, then y1
    
    for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
        for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
            center_x = x1 + a * max_k // 2
            center_y = y1 + b * max_k // 2
            
            dist_sq = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
            
            if dist_sq < best_dist_sq:
                best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                best_x1 = x1
                best_y1 = y1
            elif dist_sq == best_dist_sq:
                # Lexicographical order: (x1,y1,x2,y2)
                # We want smaller x1 first, then smaller y1
                if x1 < best_x1 or (x1 == best_x1 and y1 < best_y1):
                    best_x1 = x1
                    best_y1 = y1
    
    x2 = x1 + a * max_k
    y2 = y1 + b * max_k
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, x2, y2)

solve()
```

- So we need x - a*k/2 ≥ 0 → k ≤ 2x/a

```python
    best_x1, best_y1, best_dist_sq = 0, 0, float('inf')
    
    # We need to consider k from 0 to max_possible_k
    # But we want to find the one that minimizes center distance
    
    for k in range(0, max_possible_k + 1):
        # For this k, valid ranges
        x1_min = max(0, x - a * k)
        x1_max = min(x, n - a * k)
        y1_min = max(0, y - b * k)
        y1_max = min(y, m - b * k)
        
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # Optimal placement for center at (x,y)
        # x1_opt = x - a*k/2, y1_opt = y - b*k/2
        x1_opt = x - a * k // 2
        y1_opt = y - b * k // 2
        
        # Find closest valid x1 and y1
        x1_candidate = x1_opt
        if x1_candidate < x1_min:
            x1_candidate = x1_min
        elif x1_candidate > x1_max:
            x1_candidate = x1_max
            
        y1_candidate = y1_opt
        if y1_candidate < y1_min:
            y1_candidate = y1_min
        elif y1_candidate > y1_max:
            y1_candidate = y1_max
            
        # Check if there are better candidates within range
        # We need to check the boundaries
        candidates = []
        for dx in [-1, 0, 1]:
            x1_t = x1_candidate + dx
            if x1_t < x1_min or x1_t > x1_max:
                continue
            candidates.append((x1_t, y1_candidate))
        
        for dy in [-1, 0, 1]:
            y1_t = y1_candidate + dy
            if y1_t < y1_min or y1_t > y1_max:
                continue
            candidates.append((x1_candidate, y1_t))
        
        # Also add the corner points
        candidates.append((x1_min, y1_min))
        candidates.append((x1_min, y1_max))
        candidates.append((x1_max, y1_min))
        candidates.append((x1_max, y1_max))
        
        # Remove duplicates and keep unique ones
        candidates = list(set(candidates))
        
        for x1_c, y1_c in candidates:
            center_x = x1_c + a * k // 2
            center_y = y1_c + b * k // 2
            dist_sq = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
            
            if dist_sq < best_dist_sq:
                best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                best_x1, best_y1 = x1_c, y1_c
            elif dist_sq == best_dist_sq:
                # Lexicographically smaller
                if x1_c < best_x1 or (x1_c == best_x1 and y1_c < best_y1):
                    best_x1, best_y1 = x1_c, y1_c
    
    x2 = best_x1 + a * max_possible_k
    y2 = best_y1 + b * max_possible_k
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, x2, y2)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output

Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
