Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0≤x≤n, 0≤y≤m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b
   - Maximizes the area (or equivalently, maximizes the dimensions)
   - If multiple solutions, choose the one closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance
   - If still tied, choose lexicographically smallest

## Mathematical Analysis

Given the ratio requirement: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b

This means: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)

We want to maximize the area = (x₂-x₁)(y₂-y₁).

Let's denote:
- w = x₂ - x₁ (width)
- h = y₂ - y₁ (height)

So: w/h = a/b, which gives us: w = (a/b) × h

The area is w×h = (a/b)×h².

To maximize area, we need to maximize h (since a and b are fixed).

## Constraints

For the rectangle to contain (x,y):
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ → x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + w
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ → y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + h

So:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + w → 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ x
- x₁ + w ≥ x → x₁ ≥ x - w
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + h → 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ y
- y₁ + h ≥ y → y₁ ≥ y - h

## Solving the Problem

1. For a given height h, width w = (a×h)/b
2. Since w must be integer, h must be divisible by b (or we need to find h such that a×h/b is integer)
3. We need the largest possible h such that we can place the rectangle containing (x,y)

Actually, we can think of it differently:
- The rectangle has width w and height h such that w/h = a/b
- This means w = k×a and h = k×b for some positive integer k
- We want to maximize k

## Finding Valid k Values

For a given k:
- Width = k×a
- Height = k×b

Constraints:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a → x₁ ≥ max(0, x - k×a) and x₁ ≤ min(x, x)
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k×b → y₁ ≥ max(0, y - k×b) and y₁ ≤ min(y, y)

For valid placement, we need:
- x₁ ≥ 0 and x₁ ≤ x
- x₁ ≥ x - k×a and x₁ ≤ x
- y₁ ≥ 0 and y₁ ≤ y  
- y₁ ≥ y - k×b and y₁ ≤ y

So we need:
- max(0, x - k×a) ≤ x
- max(0, y - k×b) ≤ y

And for the rectangle to fit:
- x₁ + k×a ≤ n → x₁ ≤ n - k×a
- y₁ + k×b ≤ m → y₁ ≤ m - k×b

So we need:
- x₁ ≥ max(0, x - k×a) and x₁ ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
- y₁ ≥ max(0, y - k×b) and y₁ ≤ min(y, m - k×b)

For a valid k, we need:
- max(0, x - k×a) ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
- max(0, y - k×b) ≤ min(y, m - k×b)

This simplifies to:
- x - k×a ≤ n - k×a (always true when x ≤ n)
- x - k×a ≤ x (always true)
- k×a ≤ n (so k ≤ n/a)
- k×b ≤ m (so k ≤ m/b)

And:
- k×a ≤ n
- k×b ≤ m

So maximum possible k is min(n//a, m//b)

We want the maximum k to maximize the area.

## Finding Optimal Placement

For maximum k = min(n//a, m//b), we have:
- w = k×a
- h = k×b

We need to place the rectangle such that it contains (x,y).

To minimize Euclidean distance to center:
- Center of rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁ + x₁ + w)/2, (y₁ + y₁ + h)/2)
- Distance squared = ((x₁ + w/2 - x)² + (y₁ + h/2 - y)²)

We want to minimize this.

Constraints:
- x₁ ≥ max(0, x - w)
- x₁ ≤ min(x, n - w)
- y₁ ≥ max(0, y - h)
- y₁ ≤ min(y, m - h)

For lexicographic order, we try to minimize x₁ first, then y₁.

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Maximum possible k such that k*a <= n and k*b <= m
max_k = min(n // a, m // b)

if max_k == 0:
    # No valid sub-rectangle exists
    print(0, 0, 0, 0)
else:
    k = max_k
    w = k * a
    h = k * b
    
    # Find valid ranges for x1 and y1
    x1_min = max(0, x - w)
    x1_max = min(x, n - w)
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - h)
    y1_max = min(y, m - h)
    
    # If no valid placement, find the maximum possible k
    # Actually, with our calculation of max_k, there should be a valid placement
    
    # We want to minimize Euclidean distance to (x,y)
    # Distance squared = (x1 + w/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + h/2 - y)^2
    
    # First, find x1 that minimizes distance
    # For fixed y1, we want x1 to be as close to x - w/2 as possible
    
    x1_opt = x1_min
    y1_opt = y1_min
    
    # We need to check if we can get closer
    # Try to minimize distance directly
    
    # Find the best x1
    x1_best = x1_min
    for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
        # For each x1, find best y1
        for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
            center_x = (x1 + x1 + w) / 2
            center_y = (y1 + y1 + h) / 2
            dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
            
            # Update if better
            if dist_sq < dist_sq or (dist_sq == dist_sq and (x1, y1) < (x1_best, y1_opt)):
                x1_best = x1
                y1_opt = y1
    
    # But the ranges might be huge, so we need a smarter approach
    
    # For minimum distance, x1 should be as close as possible to x - w/2
    # For y1 should be as close as possible to y - h/2
    
    x1_target = x - w / 2
    y1_target = y - h / 2
    
    x1_candidate = x1_min
    y1_candidate = y1_min
    
    # We should try the values that are closest to the targets
    x1_candidate = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, int(x1_target)))
    # For lexicographic, we take the minimum possible x1 first, then min y1
    
    # Actually, lexicographic minimum means we want smallest x1 first, then smallest y1
    # But we also want closest to center
    
    # Find all valid (x1, y1) and pick the best
    
    # Since ranges might be large, we need to be smart
    # For each x1 in [x1_min, x1_max], find the best y1
    
    best_x1 = x1_min
    best_y1 = y1_min
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Try all x1 values - but this might be too slow
    # We can optimize by noticing that for fixed x1, y1 should be as close as possible to y - h/2
    
    for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
        y1_target = y - h / 2
        y1_candidate = y1_min
        y1_candidate2 = y1_max
        
        # Find y1 that minimizes distance
        y1_opt = y1_min
        for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
            center_x = (x1 + x1 + w) / 2
            center_y = (y1 + y1 + h) / 2
            dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
            
            if dist_sq < best_dist_sq or (dist_sq == best_dist_sq and (x1, y1) < (best_x1, best_y1)):
                best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                best_x1 = x1
                best_y1 = y1
    
    # Output
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x1 + w, best_y1 + h)
```

Wait, this approach might be too slow for large ranges. Let me reconsider.

For the center to be closest to (x,y), the center should be as close as possible to (x,y). The center is at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2).

So we want to minimize |(x1 + w/2) - x| + |(y1 + h/2) - y| for the Euclidean distance, but it's actually the sum of squares.

A better approach:
- For each valid x1, the optimal y1 is the one closest to y - h/2 in the valid range
- For each valid y1, the optimal x1 is the one closest to x - w/2 in the valid range

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Maximum possible k such that k*a <= n and k*b <= m
max_k = min(n // a, m // b)

if max_k == 0:
    print(0, 0, 0, 0)
else:
    k = max_k
    w = k * a
    h = k * b
    
    # Find valid ranges for x1 and y1
    x1_min = max(0, x - w)
    x1_max = min(x, n - w)
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - h)
    y1_max = min(y, m - h)
    
    # If no valid range, we have a problem
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        # This shouldn't happen with our max_k calculation
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
    else:
        # Find best (x1, y1) to minimize distance to (x,y)
        best_x1 = x1_min
        best_y1 = y1_min
        best_dist_sq = float('inf')
        
        # Try all possible x1 values
        for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
            # For this x1, find best y1
            target_y = y - h / 2
            best_y_for_x1 = y1_min
            dist_for_x1 = float('inf')
            
            for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
                center_x = x1 + w / 2
                center_y = y1 + h / 2
                dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                
                if dist_sq < dist_for_x1 or (dist_sq == dist_for_x1 and (x1, y1) < (best_x1, best_y1)):
                    dist_for_x1 = dist_sq
                    best_y_for_x1 = y1
                    
            if dist_for_x1 < best_dist_sq or (dist_for_x1 == best_dist_sq and (x1, best_y_for_x1) < (best_x1, best_y1)):
                best_dist_sq = dist_for_x1
                best_x1 = x1
                best_y1 = best_y_for_x1
                
        print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x1 + w, best_y1 + h)
```

But this is still potentially too slow. Let me optimize further.

Actually, for the given constraints of n,m up to 10^9, the ranges x1_min to x1_max and y1_min to y1_max could be huge. We need a smarter approach.

The key insight: the optimal x1 is either at the boundary or at the value closest to (x - w/2). Similarly for y1.

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Maximum possible k such that k*a <= n and k*b <= m
max_k = min(n // a, m // b)

if max_k == 0:
    print(0, 0, 0, 0)
else:
    k = max_k
    w = k * a
    h = k * b
    
    # Find valid ranges for x1 and y1
    x1_min = max(0, x - w)
    x1_max = min(x, n - w)
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - h)
    y1_max = min(y, m - h)
    
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
    else:
        # For optimal placement, we want center as close as possible to (x,y)
        # Center is at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
        
        # Try candidate values for x1 and y1
        candidates = []
        
        # Try the boundaries and values closest to target
        x1_candidates = [x1_min, x1_max]
        y1_candidates = [y1_min, y1_max]
        
        # Also try the values closest to x - w/2 and y - h/2
        target_x1 = x - w / 2
        target_y1 = y - h / 2
        
        x1_candidates.append(max(x1_min, min(x1_max, int(target_x1))))
        y1_candidates.append(max(y1_min, min(y1_max, int(target_y1))))
        
        # For lexicographic order, we'll consider all combinations
        # but we need to evaluate distance
        
        best_x1 = x1_min
        best_y1 = y1_min
        best_dist_sq = float('inf')
        
        # Try the boundary values
        for x1 in [x1_min, x1_max]:
            if x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max:
                for y1 in [y1_min, y1_max]:
                    if y1_min <= y1 <= y1_max:
                        center_x = x1 + w / 2
                        center_y = y1 + h / 2
                        dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                        
                        if dist_sq < best_dist_sq or (dist_sq == best_dist_sq and (x1, y1) < (best_x1, best_y1)):
                            best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                            best_x1 = x1
                            best_y1 = y1
        
        # Try the target values
        for x1 in [max(x1_min, min(x1_max, int(target_x1))), max(x1_min, min(x1_max, int(target_x1))) + 1]:
            if x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max:
                for y1 in [max(y1_min, min(y1_max, int(target_y1))), max(y1_min, min(y1_max, int(target_y1))) + 1]:
                    if y1_min <= y1 <= y1_max:
                        center_x = x1 + w / 2
                        center_y = y1 + h / 2
                        dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                        
                        if dist_sq < best_dist_sq or (dist_sq == best_dist_sq and (x1, y1) < (best_x1, best_y1)):
                            best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                            best_x1 = x1
                            best_y1 = y1
        
        print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x1 + w, best_y1 + h)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Ma5termind and Subway are bored of their normal life.They want to do something interesting so that they can enjoy their last sem of college life.As usual Ma5termind comes up with a simple and interesting game.

Ma5termind gives Subway a compressed string. A compressed String is composed of characters and numbers.Every character in a compressed string contains a number. Ex a3b1c2 it represents aaabcc. Other compressed string could be a22b2a1 , z5c2x36. Each number in the compressed string indicates the number of times the corresponding character occurs in the string. The final string is 1 indexed.  Ma5termind wants Subway to sort the compressed string and tell him the kth character in the sorted compressed string.Since subway got speed Ma5termind asks him various such questions.

Help Subway in answering Ma5termind's question.

Input:

The first line contains the compressed string.This is followed by Q that indicates the number of questions Ma5termind shoots on Subway.Follows Q lines each line contain an integer K.

Output:

For every question output the Kth  character if it exists else print -1 .

Constraints:

2 ≤ Length of Compressed String ≤ 10^4

1 ≤ Length of Final String ≤ 10^18

1 ≤ Q ≤ 10^5

1 ≤ K ≤ 10^18

Every character in the string is lowercase English letter.

Note:

The number for a character may contain leading zeroes.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the final string lexicographically (alphabetically)
3. For each query K, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the Compressed String
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- The pattern is: character followed by number(s)

### Step 2: Build the Final String
- For each character, repeat it according to its frequency
- Sort the characters alphabetically

### Step 3: Handle Queries
- For each query K, check if K is within the valid range
- If yes, return the Kth character; if no, return -1

### Step 4: Optimization Consideration
- The final string length can be up to 10^18, so we can't build the actual string
- Instead, we need to calculate which character corresponds to position K using the cumulative frequencies

## Implementation Approach

1. Parse the compressed string to get (character, frequency) pairs
2. Sort pairs by character
3. For each query K, find which character block contains position K
4. Return that character (or -1 if K is out of range)

```python
def parse_compressed_string(s):
    """Parse compressed string into list of (char, count) pairs"""
    result = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(s):
        char = s[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Parse the number following the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < len(s) and s[i].isdigit():
            num_str += s[i]
            i += 1
        
        if num_str:
            count = int(num_str)
            result.append((char, count))
    
    return result

def solve():
    # Read input
    compressed_string = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string
    char_freq = parse_compressed_string(compressed_string)
    
    # Sort by character (alphabetical order)
    char_freq.sort()
    
    # Calculate cumulative frequencies to answer queries
    # We don't need to build the actual string since it can be very long
    
    # Find the total length of the final string
    total_length = sum(freq for _, freq in char_freq)
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is valid
        if k <= 0 or k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character block contains position k
            current_pos = 0
            for char, freq in char_freq:
                if current_pos + freq >= k:
                    # K falls within this character's range
                    print(char)
                    break
                current_pos += freq
            else:
                # This shouldn't happen due to our validation
                print(-1)

solve()
```

## How it works:

1. **Parse**: Extract each character and its frequency from the compressed string
2. **Sort**: Arrange characters in alphabetical order
3. **Query handling**: For each query K:
   - Check if K is within valid range (1 to total length)
   - Traverse the sorted character list, accumulating frequencies
   - When we find a character whose cumulative frequency range contains K, that's our answer

## Time Complexity:
- O(n + q log n) where n is length of compressed string, q is number of queries
- The sorting dominates the complexity

## Space Complexity:
- O(n) for storing the parsed character frequencies

The solution efficiently handles the large constraints without building the actual decompressed string of potentially massive size (up to 10^18 characters).

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort this expanded string lexicographically
3. For each query K, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle cases where numbers have leading zeros (like "a22b2a1")

### Step 2: Sort the characters
- Sort the characters lexicographically (a < b < c < ...)

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- Since the final string can be very long (up to 10^18), we can't generate the entire string
- We need to use prefix sum approach to find the Kth character without constructing the entire string

### Step 4: Implementation plan
1. Parse the input string to get character frequencies
2. Sort the characters by their ASCII values (lexicographic order)
3. For each query K, find which character it corresponds to by checking prefix sums of frequencies

## Code Implementation

```python
def parse_compressed_string(s):
    """Parse compressed string and return list of (char, count) tuples"""
    result = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(s):
        char = s[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number that follows
        num_str = ""
        while i < len(s) and s[i].isdigit():
            num_str += s[i]
            i += 1
        
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        result.append((char, count))
    
    return result

def solve():
    # Read input
    compressed_string = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string
    char_frequencies = parse_compressed_string(compressed_string)
    
    # Sort characters lexicographically
    char_frequencies.sort()
    
    # Calculate prefix sums for efficient k-th query lookup
    # prefix_sum[i] = total count of characters from index 0 to i
    prefix_sum = []
    total_count = 0
    
    for char, count in char_frequencies:
        total_count += count
        prefix_sum.append(total_count)
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is valid
        if k > total_count:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character corresponds to position k
            # Binary search or linear search through prefix sums
            char_idx = 0
            for i, cumulative_count in enumerate(prefix_sum):
                if k <= cumulative_count:
                    print(char_frequencies[i][0])
                    break
                char_idx = i

solve()
```

## Explanation of the Algorithm

1. **Parsing**: We iterate through the string, extract each character, and then extract the following digits to get the count.

2. **Sorting**: We sort the characters lexicographically (alphabetically) since this is how we want to sort the final string.

3. **Prefix sums**: We calculate the cumulative frequency of each character in lexicographic order. This allows us to quickly determine which character the Kth position belongs to.

4. **Query processing**: For each query K, we simply check if K is within the total count. If yes, we find the first prefix sum that is >= K, and that tells us which character is at position K.

## Time Complexity
- Parsing: O(n) where n is the length of compressed string
- Sorting: O(m log m) where m is the number of unique characters (m ≤ 26)
- Prefix sums: O(m)
- Query processing: O(q log m) due to binary search in prefix sums (though here we do linear search which is fine as m is small)

## Space Complexity
- O(m) for storing character frequencies and prefix sums

The solution efficiently handles the large constraint of up to 10^18 for K by not constructing the full string, just determining which character belongs to position K.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the final string lexicographically (alphabetically)
3. For each query K, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers that can have leading zeros (like "22" or "36")

### Step 2: Build the final string
- Group characters by frequency and sort them lexicographically
- Create a list of (character, count) pairs

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- Since the final string can be very long (up to 10^18), we can't build the entire string
- We need to find the Kth character without generating all characters
- For each character, calculate how many positions it occupies
- Find which character contains the Kth position

### Step 4: Implementation approach
1. Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
2. Sort characters alphabetically
3. For each query K:
   - Calculate cumulative counts until we find which character contains position K
   - Return that character, or -1 if K exceeds total length

## Code Implementation

```python
def parse_compressed_string(s):
    """Parse compressed string and return dict of char -> count"""
    result = {}
    i = 0
    while i < len(s):
        char = s[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number that follows
        num_str = ""
        while i < len(s) and s[i].isdigit():
            num_str += s[i]
            i += 1
        
        if num_str:
            count = int(num_str)
            result[char] = result.get(char, 0) + count
    
    return result

def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_counts = parse_compressed_string(compressed)
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_counts.keys())
    
    # Calculate total length of the final string
    total_length = sum(char_counts[char] for char in sorted_chars)
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # If K exceeds total length, return -1
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find which character contains the k-th position
        current_pos = 0
        for char in sorted_chars:
            count = char_counts[char]
            if current_pos + count >= k:
                # The k-th character is in this character group
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += count
        else:
            # This should not happen since we checked k <= total_length
            print(-1)

solve()
```

## How it works:

1. **Parse function**: Goes through each character and extracts the following number (can have multiple digits)
2. **Character grouping**: Builds a dictionary of character -> count
3. **Sorting**: Sorts characters alphabetically since lexicographic order is what we want
4. **Query processing**: 
   - First checks if K exceeds total length
   - Then iterates through sorted characters, accumulating counts until we find which character contains position K
   - Returns that character

## Time Complexity:
- O(n + m log m + q * m) where n is compressed string length, m is number of unique characters, q is number of queries
- The sorting is O(m log m) and each query is O(m)

## Space Complexity:
- O(m) for storing character counts and sorted characters

The algorithm efficiently handles large values of K (up to 10^18) without constructing the entire string.

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
def parse_compressed_string(s):
    """Parse compressed string and return dict of char -> count"""
    result = {}
    i = 0
    while i < len(s):
        char = s[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number that follows
        num_str = ""
        while i < len(s) and s[i].isdigit():
            num_str += s[i]
            i += 1
        
        if num_str:
            count = int(num_str)
            result[char] = result.get(char, 0) + count
    
    return result

def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_counts = parse_compressed_string(compressed)
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_counts.keys())
    
    # Calculate total length of the final string
    total_length = sum(char_counts[char] for char in sorted_chars)
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # If K exceeds total length, return -1
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find which character contains the k-th position
        current_pos = 0
        for char in sorted_chars:
            count = char_counts[char]
            if current_pos + count >= k:
                # The k-th character is in this character group
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += count
        else:
            # This should not happen since we checked k <= total_length
            print(-1)

solve()

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Ma5termind and Subway are bored of their normal life.They want to do something interesting so that they can enjoy their last sem of college life.As usual Ma5termind comes up with a simple and interesting game.

Ma5termind gives Subway a compressed string. A compressed String is composed of characters and numbers.Every character in a compressed string contains a number. Ex a3b1c2 it represents aaabcc. Other compressed string could be a22b2a1 , z5c2x36. Each number in the compressed string indicates the number of times the corresponding character occurs in the string. The final string is 1 indexed.  Ma5termind wants Subway to sort the compressed string and tell him the kth character in the sorted compressed string.Since subway got speed Ma5termind asks him various such questions.

Help Subway in answering Ma5termind's question.

Input:

The first line contains the compressed string.This is followed by Q that indicates the number of questions Ma5termind shoots on Subway.Follows Q lines each line contain an integer K.

Output:

For every question output the Kth  character if it exists else print -1 .

Constraints:

2 ≤ Length of Compressed String ≤ 10^4

1 ≤ Length of Final String ≤ 10^18

1 ≤ Q ≤ 10^5

1 ≤ K ≤ 10^18

Every character in the string is lowercase English letter.

Note:

The number for a character may contain leading zeroes.

SAMPLE INPUT
a2b3c2a1

4

1

8


SAMPLE OUTPUT
a

b

a

c

The final sorted string is aaabbbcc.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort this expanded string (which is naturally sorted by character frequency)
3. For Q queries, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers that might have leading zeros (like "22")

### Step 2: Create frequency map
- Go through the string and parse each character-frequency pair
- Store how many times each character appears

### Step 3: Sort characters
- Sort characters alphabetically since the final string is 1-indexed and sorted

### Step 4: Answer queries efficiently
- Since the total length can be up to 10^18, we can't generate the entire string
- We need to use prefix sums to quickly find the Kth character
- For each character, calculate the prefix sum of frequencies
- Find which character contains the Kth position

### Step 5: Handle edge cases
- If K exceeds the total length, return -1

## Implementation

```python
def parse_compressed_string(s):
    """Parse the compressed string and return character frequencies"""
    freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(s)
    
    while i < n:
        # Get character
        char = s[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Parse the number following the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and s[i].isdigit():
            num_str += s[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Add to frequency map
        if count > 0:
            freq[char] = freq.get(char, 0) + count
    
    return freq

def solve():
    # Read input
    compressed_str = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse compressed string to get frequencies
    freq = parse_compressed_string(compressed_str)
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(freq.keys())
    
    # Calculate prefix sums for efficient querying
    prefix_sum = []
    total_length = 0
    
    for char in sorted_chars:
        count = freq[char]
        total_length += count
        
        # Store prefix sum (cumulative frequency up to this character)
        prefix_sum.append(total_length)
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is valid
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character contains the kth position
            # Binary search or linear scan since the list is small
            for i, prefix in enumerate(prefix_sum):
                if k <= prefix:
                    # The kth character is the current character
                    print(sorted_chars[i])
                    break

solve()
```

## How it works:

1. **Parse**: The function parses the compressed string by iterating through each character and extracting the following number (handling multi-digit numbers)
2. **Frequency map**: Stores how many times each character appears
3. **Sorting**: Characters are sorted alphabetically as they naturally appear in the final string
4. **Prefix sums**: We calculate cumulative frequencies so we can quickly determine which segment of the string contains position K
5. **Query processing**: For each query K, we check if it's within bounds and find which character it belongs to using the prefix sums

## Time Complexity:
- Parse: O(n) where n is length of compressed string
- Sorting: O(c log c) where c is number of distinct characters (c ≤ 26)
- Queries: O(Q) with O(1) per query after preprocessing

## Space Complexity:
- O(c) for storing frequencies and sorted characters

The solution efficiently handles the large constraint of up to 10^18 total length by using prefix sums instead of generating the entire string.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which means "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the final string and answer Q queries about the Kth character
3. The final string is sorted lexicographically
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle cases where numbers have leading zeros (like "a22b2a1")

### Step 2: Generate the final string
- Create a list of (character, frequency) pairs
- Sort by character (lexicographically)

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- Since the final string can be very long (up to 10^18), we can't generate it completely
- We need to find the Kth character without generating the entire string
- We can use binary search on the character set or directly calculate based on cumulative frequencies

### Step 4: Implementation approach
1. Parse the input to get character frequencies
2. Sort characters lexicographically
3. For each query K, find which character it belongs to by checking cumulative frequencies
4. If K is within bounds, return that character; otherwise return -1

```python
def parse_compressed_string(s):
    """Parse compressed string and return character frequencies"""
    char_freq = {}
    i = 0
    while i < len(s):
        char = s[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number that follows
        num_str = ""
        while i < len(s) and s[i].isdigit():
            num_str += s[i]
            i += 1
        
        if num_str:
            count = int(num_str)
            char_freq[char] = char_freq.get(char, 0) + count
    
    return char_freq

def solve():
    # Read input
    compressed_string = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get frequencies
    char_freq = parse_compressed_string(compressed_string)
    
    # Sort characters lexicographically
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_freq.keys())
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is valid
        total_length = sum(char_freq.values())
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find which character the Kth position belongs to
        current_pos = 0
        for char in sorted_chars:
            freq = char_freq[char]
            if current_pos + freq >= k:
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += freq
        else:
            # This should not happen if we checked validity
            print(-1)

solve()
```

## How it works:

1. **Parse function**: Iterates through the string, extracting characters and their frequencies. It handles numbers with leading zeros naturally since we just concatenate digits.

2. **Query handling**: For each query K:
   - First check if K exceeds the total length of the final string
   - Then iterate through sorted characters, accumulating frequencies until we find the character that contains the Kth position
   - Return that character

3. **Efficiency**: Time complexity is O(Q * n log n) where n is the number of unique characters (at most 26), making it efficient even for large Q.

The solution efficiently handles the constraints where the final string length can be up to 10^18 by avoiding actual string generation and using frequency counting.

- Create a frequency map of characters and their counts
- Sort characters alphabetically

- Instead, we need to find which character corresponds to position K

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number part
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        freq[char] = freq.get(char, 0) + count
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(freq.keys())
    
    # Total length of the final string
    total_length = sum(freq.values())
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is valid
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue

        current_pos = 0
        
        for char in sorted_chars:
            count = freq[char]
            # If current position + count >= k, then kth character is this char

                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += count
        else:
            # This should not happen since we checked k <= total_length
            print(-1)

solve()
```

## How it works:

1. **Parsing**: We iterate through the compressed string, extracting each character followed by its count (which can be multi-digit).

2. **Frequency mapping**: We build a dictionary where keys are characters and values are their total occurrences.

3. **Query processing**: For each query K:
   - First check if K exceeds total length (return -1 if so)
   - Iterate through characters in alphabetical order
   - For each character, check if the current position + its count covers position K
   - If yes, that character is at position K
   - If no, move to the next character

4. **Efficiency**: This solution works in O(Q × C log C) where C is the number of unique characters, which is efficient even for large K values up to 10^18.

The time complexity is acceptable since we don't need to construct the entire string - we just need to determine which character corresponds to position K through cumulative frequency calculations.

3. For each query K, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted string

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their counts
- Handle numbers that may have leading zeros (like "22" or "36")

- For each character with count, add that many instances
- Sort the characters alphabetically

### Step 3: Handle queries
- For each query K, check if K is within bounds
- Return the Kth character if exists, otherwise return -1

### Step 4: Optimization consideration
- Since the final string length can be up to 10^18, we cannot generate the entire string
- We need to calculate the Kth character without building the entire string

## Key Insight
Instead of building the entire string, we can:
1. Sort the characters by their letter
2. For each character, calculate how many positions it occupies
3. Use binary search or direct lookup to find the Kth character

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get (character, count) pairs
    chars = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(compressed):
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number part
        num_str = ""
        while i < len(compressed) and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        if num_str:
            count = int(num_str)
            chars.append((char, count))
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    chars.sort()
    
    # Calculate prefix sums to find the Kth character without building the string
    prefix_sum = []
    total_length = 0
    
    for char, count in chars:
        total_length += count
        prefix_sum.append(total_length)
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is within bounds
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character group contains the Kth position
            # Using binary search or direct lookup
            found = False
            for idx, (char, count) in enumerate(chars):
                if k <= count:
                    print(char)
                    found = True
                    break
                k -= count
            
            if not found:
                print(-1)

solve()
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Ma5termind and Subway are bored of their normal life.They want to do something interesting so that they can enjoy their last sem of college life.As usual Ma5termind comes up with a simple and interesting game.

Ma5termind gives Subway a compressed string. A compressed String is composed of characters and numbers.Every character in a compressed string contains a number. Ex a3b1c2 it represents aaabcc. Other compressed string could be a22b2a1 , z5c2x36. Each number in the compressed string indicates the number of times the corresponding character occurs in the string. The final string is 1 indexed.  Ma5termind wants Subway to sort the compressed string and tell him the kth character in the sorted compressed string.Since subway got speed Ma5termind asks him various such questions.

Help Subway in answering Ma5termind's question.

b

a

c

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which represents "aaabbbcc" (a appears 2+1=3 times, b appears 3 times, c appears 2 times)
2. We need to sort the final string lexicographically (alphabetically)
3. For Q queries, we need to find the K-th character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Go through each character in the compressed string
- Extract the character and the number that follows it
- Count how many times each character appears

### Step 2: Sort the characters
- Sort the characters alphabetically (a to z)

### Step 3: Handle queries efficiently
- Since the final string can be very long (up to 10^18), we can't generate the entire string
- For each query K, we need to find which character is at position K
- We can do this by iterating through the sorted characters and checking if K falls within the range of that character's count

### Step 4: Implementation approach
1. Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
2. Sort the characters by their frequency
3. For each query K, iterate through the sorted characters and find which character's range contains K
4. If K exceeds the total length, return -1

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_count = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number that follows
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number string to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Update character count
        if char in char_count:
            char_count[char] += count
        else:
            char_count[char] = count
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_count.keys())
    
    # Calculate total length of the final string
    total_length = sum(char_count.values())
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is within valid range
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
        
        # Find which character is at position k
        current_position = 0
        for char in sorted_chars:
            chars_needed = char_count[char]
            if current_position + chars_needed >= k:
                # K falls within this character's range
                print(char)
                break
            current_position += chars_needed
        else:
            # This shouldn't happen since we checked k <= total_length
            print(-1)

solve()
```

## Verification with Sample Input

- Parse: a(2), b(3), c(2), a(1)
- Character counts: a:3, b:3, c:2
- Sorted characters: a, b, c
- Final string: "aaabbbcc"

Queries:
- K=2: 2nd character is 'a' ✓
- K=5: 5th character is 'b' ✓  
- K=1: 1st character is 'a' ✓
- K=8: 8th character is 'c' ✓

The solution correctly handles the problem requirements and efficiently answers queries without generating the entire string.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

The function `ZeroShark(X)` counts the number of adjacent pairs where both characters are '0'. It returns True only when there's exactly one such pair.

For a binary string of length N, we need to count how many strings have exactly one occurrence of "00" as a substring.

## Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**N=2:**
- "00": has one "00" at positions (0,1) → count = 1 → returns True
- "01": no "00" → count = 0 → returns False  
- "10": no "00" → count = 0 → returns False
- "11": no "00" → count = 0 → returns False

So answer is 1.

**N=3:**
- "000": has "00" at positions (0,1) and (1,2) → count = 2 → returns False
- "001": has "00" at position (0,1) → count = 1 → returns True
- "010": no "00" → count = 0 → returns False
- "100": has "00" at position (1,2) → count = 1 → returns True
- "011": no "00" → count = 0 → returns False
- "101": no "00" → count = 0 → returns False
- "110": no "00" → count = 0 → returns False
- "111": no "00" → count = 0 → returns False

So answer is 2.

## Pattern Recognition

For a string to have exactly one "00" pair, we need to place two consecutive zeros somewhere, and ensure no other "00" pairs exist.

The "00" can appear in positions:
- (i, i+1) for i from 0 to N-2

For each possible position of "00":
- If we place "00" starting at position i, we need to ensure no other "00" pairs exist
- We also need to consider that this "00" pair might create additional constraints

Let me think about valid configurations:

1. **Single "00" pair at position (i,i+1)**: 
   - Positions i and i+1 must be 0
   - For no other "00" pairs to exist:
     - If i > 0: position i-1 must not be 0 (so that (i-1,i) isn't "00")
     - If i+1 < N-1: position i+2 must not be 0 (so that (i+1,i+2) isn't "00")

2. **Edge cases**:
   - For "00" at start (i=0): position 0 and 1 are 0, no constraint on left
   - For "00" at end (i=N-2): position N-2 and N-1 are 0, no constraint on right

Let me count valid configurations:

For "00" at position (i,i+1):
- If i = 0: position 0,1 = 0, no left constraint. Right constraint: position 2 must not be 0 if N > 2
- If i = N-2: position N-2,N-1 = 0, no right constraint. Left constraint: position N-3 must not be 0 if N > 2
- For 0 < i < N-2: position i-1 ≠ 0 and position i+2 ≠ 0

But we also need to consider that having "00" at one place might be the only constraint.

Actually, let me reconsider with a simpler approach:

The string has exactly one "00" pair if and only if:
- There is exactly one position where two consecutive zeros occur
- No other consecutive zeros exist

For each possible position (i,i+1) of "00":
- i ranges from 0 to N-2
- For the "00" at (i,i+1):
  - If i > 0, then position i-1 must be 1 (to avoid "00" at (i-1,i))
  - If i+1 < N-1, then position i+2 must be 1 (to avoid "00" at (i+1,i+2))

This gives us:
- For position i: we have 1 choice for each constraint (must be 1 or must be 0)
- Remaining positions (not constrained by the "00" pair) can be either 0 or 1

Let me count:
- For each i from 0 to N-2:
  - The pattern is: (some fixed values) + "00" + (some fixed values)
  - The values are fully determined by the constraints

For position i:
- Positions 0 to i-1: if i > 0, position i-1 must be 1, others are free (if not constrained)
- Positions i+2 to N-1: if i+1 < N-1, position i+2 must be 1, others are free

Actually, let me think about this more systematically.

For "00" at position (i,i+1):
- Fix positions i and i+1 to 0
- Fix position i-1 to 1 (if i > 0)
- Fix position i+2 to 1 (if i+1 < N-1)
- All other positions can be either 0 or 1

But I need to ensure that no other "00" pairs exist. The only way another "00" could form is if we have consecutive zeros elsewhere.

Actually, the only constraints are:
- For i = 0: we only constrain position 2 to be 1 (if N > 2)
- For i = N-2: we only constrain position N-3 to be 1 (if N > 2)
- For 1 ≤ i ≤ N-3: we constrain both position i-1 and i+2 to be 1

The number of free positions is (N - number of fixed positions).

For each i:
- Fixed positions: i, i+1 (always)
- Additional fixed positions: i-1 (if i > 0), i+2 (if i+1 < N-1)

So total fixed positions = 2 + [i > 0] + [i+1 < N-1]

Number of free positions = N - (2 + [i > 0] + [i+1 < N-1])

Each free position has 2 choices, so contribution = 2^(free positions)

But I need to handle the cases:

Case 1: N = 1
- No possible "00" pairs, so answer = 0

Case 2: N = 2
- Only possible at i = 0
- No constraints on i-1 (since i=0), constraint on i+2 (but i+2 >= 2, so N-1 = 1, i+2 > N-1)
- So no additional constraints
- Free positions: 2 - 2 = 0
- Contribution = 2^0 = 1

Case 3: N = 3
- i = 0: positions 0,1 = 0; constraint at position 2 (i+2=2), so position 2 = 1
- i = 1: positions 1,2 = 0; constraint at position 0 (i-1=0), so position 0 = 1
- For i=0: fixed positions 0,1,2 → all fixed → 2^0 = 1
- For i=1: fixed positions 0,1,2 → all fixed → 2^0 = 1
- Total = 2

This matches the sample!

General formula:
For each i from 0 to N-2:
- Number of fixed positions = 2 + (1 if i > 0) + (1 if i+1 < N-1)
- Number of free positions = N - (2 + (i > 0) + (i+1 < N-1))
- Contribution = 2^(free positions)

But wait: if the free positions count is negative, it means no valid configuration. This happens when:
- i > 0 and i+1 < N-1: fixed positions = 4, free = N-4

For N=3:

- Total = 2 ✓

All have exactly one "00" pair. So answer should be 5.

I think my analysis was correct. Let me implement it.

## Solution

```python
def solve(n):
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    if n <= 1:
        return 0
    
    # Precompute powers of 2
    pow2 = [1] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        pow2[i] = (pow2[i-1] * 2) % MOD
    
    total = 0
    
    # For each possible position of "00" at (i, i+1)
    for i in range(n - 1):  # i from 0 to n-2
        # Number of fixed positions
        fixed = 2  # positions i and i+1
        
        # Constraint on left: position i-1 must be 1 if i > 0
        if i > 0:
            fixed += 1
            
        # Constraint on right: position i+2 must be 1 if i+1 < n-1
        if i + 1 < n - 1:
            fixed += 1
            
        # Number of free positions
        free = n - fixed
        
        # If free positions are negative, this configuration is impossible
        if free < 0:
            continue
            
        # Each free position has 2 choices (0 or 1)
        contribution = pow2[free]
        total = (total + contribution) % MOD
    
    return total

# Read input and solve
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    print(solve(n))
```

N=2:
- i=0: fixed = 2 (pos 0,1) + (i>0? no) + (i+1 < 1? 1 < 1? no) = 2
- free = 2-2 = 0
- contribution = 2^0 = 1
- Total = 1 ✓

N=3:
- i=0: fixed = 2 + 0 + (1 < 2? yes) = 3 → free = 0 → 1
- i=1: fixed = 2 + (1>0? yes) + (2 < 2? no) = 3 → free = 0 → 1
- Total = 2 ✓

N=4:
- i=0: fixed = 2 + 0 + (1 < 3? yes) = 3 → free = 1 → 2^1 = 2
- i=1: fixed = 2 + (1>0? yes) + (2 < 3? yes) = 4 → free = 0 → 1
- i=2: fixed = 2 + (2>0? yes) + (3 < 3? no) = 3 → free = 1 → 2^1 = 2
- Total = 2 + 1 + 2 = 5

This matches my manual count. The solution looks correct.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have binary strings of length N
2. For each string X, we run ZeroShark(X) which counts the number of consecutive "00" pairs starting from index 1 to N-1
3. ZeroShark returns True only when there's exactly one occurrence of "00" substring
4. We need to count how many such strings have exactly one "00" pair

## Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

For N=2:
- "00": has one "00" at position 1 → counter = 1 → True
- "01": no "00" → counter = 0 → False  
- "10": no "00" → counter = 0 → False
- "11": no "00" → counter = 0 → False
Result: 1 string

For N=3:
- "001": has "00" at position 1 → counter = 1 → True
- "010": no "00" → counter = 0 → False
- "100": has "00" at position 2 → counter = 1 → True
- Others have 0 or more than 1 "00" pairs
Result: 2 strings

## Pattern Recognition

For a string to have exactly one "00" pair:
- The "00" pair must occur at exactly one position (i, i+1) where 1 ≤ i ≤ N-1
- This means we have exactly one "00" consecutive pair
- The rest of the string must not contain any other "00" pairs

Let me think about the positions where we can place exactly one "00" pair:

1. **Case 1: "00" at position i (positions i and i+1)**

   - This means no other adjacent "00" pairs

2. **Constraints on other positions:**
   - For positions before i: we need to ensure no "00" pair

Yes!

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    T = int(input())
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        
        if N == 1:
            print(0)
        elif N == 2:
            print(1)
        elif N == 3:
            print(2)
        else:
            # For N >= 4: (N+1) * 2^(N-4) mod MOD
            power_of_2 = pow(2, N - 4, MOD)
            result = (N + 1) * power_of_2 % MOD
            print(result)

solve()
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have binary strings of length N
2. For each string X, we run ZeroShark(X) which counts the number of adjacent "00" pairs
3. ZeroShark returns True only when there's exactly one "00" pair
4. We need to count how many binary strings have exactly one "00" pair

## Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**N=2:**
- "00": has one "00" at position (1,0) → count = 1 → True
- "01": no "00" → count = 0 → False  
- "10": no "00" → count = 0 → False
- "11": no "00" → count = 0 → False
- Answer: 1

**N=3:**
- "000": has "00" at (1,0) and (2,1) → count = 2 → False
- "001": has "00" at (1,0) → count = 1 → True
- "010": no "00" → count = 0 → False
- "100": has "00" at (2,1) → count = 1 → True
- "011": no "00" → count = 0 → False
- "101": no "00" → count = 0 → False
- "110": no "00" → count = 0 → False
- "111": no "00" → count = 0 → False
- Answer: 2

## Pattern Recognition

For a string to have exactly one "00" pair:
- The "00" pair must occur at some position i (where 0 ≤ i ≤ N-2)
- All positions before i must be 1 or have no 0s that would create additional "00" pairs
- All positions after i must not contain "00" pairs

Let me think about valid configurations:

1. **"00" at position i**: This means x[i] = '0' and x[i+1] = '0'
2. To avoid additional "00" pairs:
   - All positions before i: if x[j] = '0' for j < i, then x[j] must be '1' (since if x[j] = '0', then we need x[j+1] = '1' to avoid forming "00")
   - All positions after i+1: if x[k] = '0' for k > i+1, then x[k-1] must be '1' to avoid forming "00"

Actually, let me reconsider. For a "00" at position i (meaning x[i] and x[i+1] are both '0'):
- To avoid additional "00" pairs, we need:
  - x[i-1] ≠ '0' (if i > 0) - so x[i-1] = '1'
  - x[i+2] ≠ '0' (if i+2 < N) - so x[i+2] = '1'
- The positions in between are unconstrained except for the adjacent constraints

So for each possible position i where we place "00":
- If i = 0: we place "00" at start, need x[2] = '1' (if N > 2), and x[1] = '0', x[2] = '1' if N > 2
- If i = N-2: we place "00" at end, need x[N-3] = '1' (if N > 2), and x[N-3] = '1' if N > 2
- For i in middle: we need x[i-1] = '1', x[i+2] = '1'

Let me count valid configurations:

For each position i from 0 to N-2:
- If we place "00" at position i (x[i]='0', x[i+1]='0'):
  - If i > 0: x[i-1] must be '1' (to prevent "00" at i-1)
  - If i+2 < N: x[i+2] must be '1' (to prevent "00" at i+1)
  - All other positions can be freely assigned

So:
- For i = 0: "00" at start, if N > 2 then x[2] = '1', so we have 1 configuration
- For i = N-2: "00" at end, if N > 2 then x[N-3] = '1', so we have 1 configuration  
- For i = 1 to N-3: "00" in middle, with constraints on i-1 and i+2, so we have 1 configuration each

Wait, let me reconsider the constraints more carefully:

For each position i where we place "00":
- x[i] = '0', x[i+1] = '0'
- If i > 0: x[i-1] = '1' (to avoid "00" at position i-1)
- If i+2 < N: x[i+2] = '1' (to avoid "00" at position i+1)

This gives us:
- For i = 0: x[0]='0', x[1]='0', if N > 2 then x[2]='1'. So only if N > 2, we have a configuration.
- For i = N-2: x[N-2]='0', x[N-1]='0', if N > 2 then x[N-3]='1'. So only if N > 2, we have a configuration.
- For i = 1 to N-3: we have x[i]='0', x[i+1]='0', with x[i-1]='1' and x[i+2]='1'.

But actually, I need to count all valid strings that contain exactly one "00" pair.

Let me think differently. A binary string has exactly one "00" pair if it has exactly one occurrence of two consecutive zeros.

For each possible position of the "00" pair:
- Position 0: "00" at start → requires x[0]='0', x[1]='0'
  - For N=2: "00" → valid
  - For N>2: x[2] must be '1' to avoid another "00", and x[1]='0', x[0]='0', x[2]='1' (for positions after)
- Position 1: "00" at positions 1,2 → requires x[1]='0', x[2]='0'
  - If N>3: x[0]='1' (to avoid "00" at pos 0), and x[3]='1' (to avoid "00" at pos 2)
- Position 2: "00" at positions 2,3 → requires x[2]='0', x[3]='0'
  - If N>4: x[1]='1' and x[4]='1'

I see the pattern now.

For each position i (0 ≤ i ≤ N-2):
- We set x[i] = '0', x[i+1] = '0'
- If i > 0: x[i-1] = '1' (to prevent "00" at i-1)
- If i+2 < N: x[i+2] = '1' (to prevent "00" at i+1)
- All other positions can be anything, but actually they are constrained by the above.

Actually, let me reconsider: the constraints are only to break off "00" patterns:
- If i > 0, we need x[i-1] = '1'
- If i+2 < N, we need x[i+2] = '1'

All positions not mentioned have no constraints.

So for each valid i:
- The number of valid configurations is 1, because all other positions are fixed by constraints.

Wait, that's not right. The constraints only fix specific positions, and all other positions are free.

But actually, once we fix the constraints, the number of valid configurations for a specific i is exactly 1, because all other positions are determined.

No, that's not true. For example, if we have i=1 in a string of length 4:
- x[1]='0', x[2]='0'
- x[0]='1' (to avoid "00" at pos 0)
- x[3]='1' (to avoid "00" at pos 2)
- Position 3 is fixed, but what about other positions? Actually, in this case, all positions are fixed.

Let me check N=4:
- i=0: x[0]='0', x[1]='0', x[2]='1' → "0011"
- i=1: x[0]='1', x[1]='0', x[2]='0', x[3]='1' → "1001"
- i=2: x[1]='1', x[2]='0', x[3]='0', x[4] doesn't exist → "1100"

For each valid i, we get exactly one valid string.

So the total count is the number of valid positions i where we can place "00" such that the constraints are satisfiable.

The constraints are:
- i ≥ 0, i ≤ N-2
- i > 0 implies x[i-1] = '1' (always possible)
- i+2 < N implies x[i+2] = '1' (always possible)

So for each i from 0 to N-2, we can always place "00" at position i, and the constraints can be satisfied.

Therefore, the answer is N-1.

Let me verify:
- N=2: answer should be 1 → N-1 = 1 ✓
- N=3: answer should be 2 → N-1 = 2 ✓

Yes! This matches the examples.

## Solution

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    T = int(input())
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        # For each position i from 0 to N-2, we can have exactly one "00" pair
        # This gives us N-1 valid strings
        result = (N - 1) % MOD
        print(result)

solve()
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have binary strings of length N
2. For each string X, we run ZeroShark(X) which counts the number of positions i (from 1 to n-1) where both x[i] and x[i-1] are '0'
3. ZeroShark returns True only when this counter equals exactly 1
4. We need to count how many binary strings satisfy this condition

## Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:
- N=2: strings are "00", "01", "10", "11"
  - "00": positions 1: x[1]='0', x[0]='0' → counter = 1 → True
  - "01": positions 1: x[1]='1', x[0]='0' → no zeros → counter = 0 → False  
  - "10": positions 1: x[1]='0', x[0]='1' → no zeros → counter = 0 → False
  - "11": positions 1: x[1]='1', x[0]='1' → no zeros → counter = 0 → False
  - Result: 1 string

- N=3: strings are all 3-bit combinations
  - "001": positions 1: x[1]='0', x[0]='0' → counter +=1; position 2: x[2]='1', x[1]='0' → no zero → final counter = 1 → True
  - "100": positions 1: x[1]='0', x[0]='1' → no zero; position 2: x[2]='0', x[1]='0' → counter +=1 → final counter = 1 → True
  - Others don't have exactly one occurrence of "00"

## Pattern Recognition

For ZeroShark(X) to return True, we need exactly one occurrence of "00" in consecutive positions.

The "00" can occur at positions:
- (1,2) - indices 0,1 in 0-based indexing
- (2,3) - indices 1,2 in 0-based indexing
- (k,k+1) for k from 1 to n-2 (1-indexed) or 0 to n-3 (0-indexed)

However, we must be careful about overlapping cases and ensure we don't double-count.

Let me think systematically:

For exactly one "00" pattern, we have:
- The "00" can start at position i (0-indexed) where i ranges from 0 to n-2
- For each valid starting position of "00", we need to ensure no other "00" appears
- The rest of the string can be arbitrary except for this constraint

Wait, let me reconsider with the examples:

For N=3:
- "001": has "00" at position (0,1) → valid
- "100": has "00" at position (1,2) → valid
- "010": has no "00" → invalid  
- "110": has no "00" → invalid
- "000": has "00" at (0,1) AND (1,2) → count = 2 → invalid
- "011": no "00" → invalid
- "101": no "00" → invalid
- "111": no "00" → invalid

So we need exactly one "00" substring.

## Solution Approach

For a string of length N, we can have exactly one "00" at position i (0-indexed, where i from 0 to N-2).

For each position i where we place "00":
- Positions 0 to i-1: must not contain "00" 
- Positions i+1 to N-1: must not contain "00"
- We need to count valid configurations

But this gets complex. Let me think about edge cases:

**Case 1: N = 1**
- Only "0" and "1"
- No consecutive pairs → counter = 0
- So ZeroShark returns False for both
- Answer = 0

**Case 2: N = 2**
- Only "00" has the pattern → answer = 1

**Case 3: N = 3**
- "001" and "100" work → answer = 2

Looking at the pattern:
- N=1: 0
- N=2: 1  
- N=3: 2

This looks familiar - it seems like the answer is N-1 for N ≥ 2, and 0 for N=1.

Let me verify:
- N=4: should have 3 strings?
- Possible strings with exactly one "00":
  - "0011" → has "00" at (0,1) only → valid
  - "0010" → has "00" at (0,1) only → valid

Wait, I need to be more systematic.

Actually, let me reconsider the pattern from the examples:
- N=2 → 1
- N=3 → 2

This suggests the answer is N-1 when N ≥ 2, and 0 when N = 1.

But is this always true?

For "00" at position i (0-indexed, i from 0 to N-2):
- If we place "00" starting at position i, we need to ensure no other "00" appears
- The string can have arbitrary values elsewhere, but must avoid other "00" sequences

Actually, I think the pattern is simpler. Let me reconsider:

For each possible position where "00" can occur (from position 0 to N-2), we can have a string where:
- The "00" pattern starts at that position
- All other positions are such that no other "00" forms

After more thought, the pattern appears to be N-1 for N ≥ 2 and 0 for N = 1.

- N=2 → 1 = 2-1
- N=3 → 2 = 3-1

```python
    MOD = 1000000007

    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
```
